\(\displaystyle \inf_{\tau \in (\bar{t}, T]} | U_{\tau} (0) | = \inf_{\tau \in (\bar{t}, T]} | \omega_{\tau} (\mathrm{e}^{V_T (X_{\tau, T})}) | \geqslant \mathrm{e}^{- (\sup_{\tau \in (\bar{t}, T]} \| \Psi_T^{(\tau)} \|)^{\deg (V_T)}} > 0.\)

This contradicts the definition of \(\bar{t}\), therefore \(\bar{t} \leqslant t\), and the claim is proven.\(\Box\)

Lemma 3.14. Under the same assumptions of Theorem 3.12, \(\Psi\) is a solution of the FBSDE in (3.18) iff it is a solution of

\(\displaystyle \mathrm{d} \Psi_s = - \dot{G}_s (F_s (\Psi_s) + R_s) \mathrm{d} s + \mathrm{d} X_s, \qquad \Psi_0 = 0,\)

where \((F_t)_{t \in [0, T]} \subset \bigwedge \mathfrak{h}\) is any continuously differentiable interpolating family such that \(F_T = \mathrm{D} V_T\) and where the remainder process \(R_s\) solves the following self-consistent equation:

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{l} R_s = \int_s^T \omega_s (\mathcal{H}_r [F_r] (\Psi_r)) \mathrm{d} r + \int_s^T \omega_s (\mathrm{D} F_r (\Psi_r) \cdot \dot{G}_r R_r) \mathrm{d} r, \end{array} \) (3.22)

with

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{H}_r [F_r] := \partial_r F_r + \frac{1}{2} \mathrm{D}_{\dot{G}_r}^2 F_r + \mathrm{D} F_r \cdot \dot{G}_r F_r\)

Proof. Set \(R_t := \omega_t (\mathrm{D} V_T (\Psi_T)) - F_t (\Psi_t) = \omega_t (F_T (\Psi_T)) - F_t (\Psi_t)\). Then, by Itô's lemma, and by using that \(\dot{G}_s\) is \(\Theta\)-antisymmetric we can write:

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} R_t & = & \int_t^{T } \omega_t \left( \partial_s F_s (\Psi_s) + \frac{1}{2} \mathrm{D}_{\dot{G}_s}^2 F_s (\Psi_s) + \mathrm{D} F_s (\Psi_s) \cdot \dot{G}_s \omega_s (F_T (\Psi_T)) \right) \mathrm{d} s\\ & = & \int_t^{T } \omega_t (\mathcal{H}_s [F_s]) \mathrm{d} s + \int_t^{T } \omega_t (\mathrm{D} F_s (\Psi_s) \cdot \dot{G}_s R_s) \mathrm{d} s. \end{array} \) (3.23)

\(\Box\)

Remark 3.15. Wick-ordering renormalisation corresponds to choosing \(F_s\) as the solution of

\(\displaystyle \partial_s F_s (\Psi_s) + \frac{1}{2} \mathrm{D}_{\dot{G}_s}^2 F_s (\Psi_s) = 0,\)

since in fact \(F_s\) is a martingale, see Proposition 2.17 and Remark 3.9. In this case, controlling \(F_s\) is simple but the non-linear term \(\mathrm{D} F_r \cdot \dot{G}_r F_r\) appears in its entirety in the equation for \(R_t\). To go beyond the Wick-ordering renormalisation, we will partially include the non-linearity into the flow equation for \(F_s\), see Section 4.

3.3FBSDE for the model

We let \(\mathbb{N}_{\infty} := \mathbb{N} \cup \{ \infty \}\) and \(\mathbb{T}^d_{L, \varepsilon} := ((\varepsilon \mathbb{Z}) / (L\mathbb{Z}))^d\) be the toroidal lattice of size \(L \in \mathbb{N}_{\infty}\) and lattice spacing \(\varepsilon \in 2^{-\mathbb{N}_{\infty}}\) with the understanding that \(\mathbb{T}_{L, 0}^d := \mathbb{T}_L^d\) and \(\mathbb{T}^d_{\infty, 0} =\mathbb{R}^d\). Because we deal with spin-\(1 / 2\) fermions, for \(L \in \mathbb{N}_{\infty}\) and \(\varepsilon \in 2^{-\mathbb{N}_{\infty}}\) we introduce the Hilbert space

\(\displaystyle \mathfrak{h}_{L, \varepsilon} := L^2 (\mathbb{T}^d_{L, \varepsilon} ; \mathbb{C}^2) \oplus L^2 (\mathbb{T}^d_{L, \varepsilon} ; \mathbb{C}^2),\)

where for \(\varepsilon > 0\) the scalar product on \(L^2 (\mathbb{T}^d_{L, \varepsilon} ; \mathbb{C}^2)\) is given by

\(\displaystyle \langle f, g \rangle_{L^2 (\mathbb{T}^d_{L, \varepsilon} ; \mathbb{C}^2)} = {\sum_{\sigma = \uparrow, \downarrow}}_{} \sum_{x \in \mathbb{T}^d_{L, \varepsilon}} \varepsilon^d \overline{f_{\sigma} (x)} g_{\sigma} (x) =: {\int_{\mathbb{T}^d_{L, \varepsilon}}}_{} \overline{f (x)} \cdot g (x) \mathrm{d} x.\)

Clearly, \(\mathfrak{h}_{L, \varepsilon}\) are finite-dimensional Hilbert spaces as long as \(L \in \mathbb{N}\) and \(\varepsilon > 0\). We abridge \(\mathfrak{h}_L := \mathfrak{h}_{L, 0}\), \(\mathfrak{h} := \mathfrak{h}_{\infty}\) and note that for any finite \(L\) we can embed \(\mathfrak{h}_{L, \varepsilon} \hookrightarrow \mathfrak{h}_L\) via truncation in the Fourier series. In more detail, we let \((\mathbb{T}^d_{L, \varepsilon})^{\ast} := \left\{ k \in 2 \pi L^{- 1} \mathbb{Z}^d \, | \, | k |_{\infty} < \pi \varepsilon^{- 1} \right\}\) and \(\mathcal{F^{}}_{L, \varepsilon} : \mathfrak{h}_{L, \varepsilon} \rightarrow \ell^2 ((\mathbb{T}^d_{L, \varepsilon})^{\ast} ; \mathbb{C}^4)\) be the Fourier transform \((\mathcal{F}_{L, \varepsilon} f) (k) := \int_{\mathbb{T}^d_{L, \varepsilon}} \mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i} k x} f (x) \mathrm{d} x\). To any element \(f \in \mathfrak{h}_{L, \varepsilon}\) we associate the following element of \(\mathfrak{h}_L\),

\(\displaystyle (\mathcal{F}_{L, 0}^{- 1} \mathcal{F}_{L, \varepsilon} f) (x) := L^{- d} \sum_{k \in (\mathbb{T}^d_{L, \varepsilon})^{\ast}} \mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i} k x} (\mathcal{F}_{L, \varepsilon} f) (k) .\)

Furthermore, if we identify \(\mathbb{T}^d_L\) with the box \((- L / 2, L / 2]^d \subset \mathbb{R}^d\), we obtain the embedding

\(\displaystyle \mathfrak{h}_{L, \varepsilon} \hookrightarrow \mathfrak{h}_L \hookrightarrow \mathfrak{h},\)

that is, an element of \(\mathfrak{h}_L\) is identified with an element of \(\mathfrak{h}\) with compact support on \(\mathbb{T}_L^d\). This is important to keep in mind because, even though we need to index the GBMs using the finite-dimensional Hilbert spaces \(\mathfrak{h}_{L, \varepsilon}\) with \(L \in \mathbb{N}\) and \(\varepsilon > 0\), we want to represent such GBMs in a sufficiently large FNPS that is directly linked to the infinite-dimensional space \(\mathfrak{h}\).

On \(\mathfrak{h}\) we define the conjugation \(\Theta (v \oplus w) := (\bar{w} \oplus \bar{v})\) for any \(v \oplus w \in \mathfrak{h}\). The operator \(G\) in (1.1) is \(\Theta\)-antisymmetric and bounded for \(\gamma \leqslant d / 2\) but has an ultraviolet divergence when \(\gamma \geqslant 0\), in the sense that the kernel is singular on the diagonal. We introduce the interpolation, see also [Ste70, Riv91]:

\(\displaystyle G_t := \frac{U}{\Gamma (d / 2 - \gamma)} \left( t \boldsymbol{1}_{0 \leqslant t < 1} \int_{2^{- 2}}^{\infty} + \boldsymbol{1}_{t \geqslant 1} \int_{{2^{- 2 t}}^{}}^{\infty} \right) \zeta^{- \gamma + d / 2 - 1} \mathrm{e}^{- \zeta (\mathbb{1} - \Delta)} \mathrm{d} \zeta, \) (3.24)

where \(U := \mathbb{1} \oplus - \mathbb{1}\) and where \(\Gamma\) is the Gamma function. It is easy to see that \(G_{\infty} = G\) and that \(G_t\) is \(\Theta\)-antisymmetric and bounded.

Although it is tempting to introduce a regularisation by replacing \(\Delta\) with the lattice Laplacian, for technical reasons, we instead make the following choice. We let \(\min_{\varepsilon}\) be a smooth non-decreasing function on \(\mathbb{R}^+\) such that \(\min_{\varepsilon} (a) = a\) on \([0, \varepsilon^{- 1}]\) and \(\min_{\varepsilon} (a) = \varepsilon^{- 1}\) on \([\varepsilon^{- 1} + 1, \infty)\). In other words, \(\min_{\varepsilon} (a)\) is a smoothening of \(\varepsilon^{- 1} \wedge a\).

Definition 3.16. Let \(L \in \mathbb{N}_{\infty}\), \(\varepsilon \in 2^{-\mathbb{N}_{\infty}}\), \(\delta \in (0, 1)\) and let \(\varphi_{\delta} \in C^{\infty}_c ([0, \infty))\) be a \(\delta^{- 1}\)-Gevrey function

3.1. We thus have \(\sup_{x \in \mathbb{R}^+} | \partial_{\nu}^n \varphi_{\delta} (x) | \leqslant C^{1 + n} (n!)^{\delta^{- 1}}\), see [GMR21, Rod93].

3.1 such that \(\operatorname{supp} (\varphi_{\delta}) = [0, 2]\) and \(\varphi_{\delta} ([0, 1]) = 1\). We define \(G_t^{L, \varepsilon}\) as the operator on \(\mathfrak{h}\) with kernel

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} G_t^{L, \varepsilon} (y ; x) & := & \frac{U}{\Gamma (d / 2 - \gamma)} \left( t \boldsymbol{1}_{0 \leqslant t < 1} \int_{2^{- 2}}^{\infty} + \boldsymbol{1}_{t \geqslant 1} \int_{1 / \min_{\varepsilon} (2^t)^2}^{\infty} \right)\\ & & \hspace{5em} L^{- d} \sum_{k \in (\mathbb{T}^d_{L, \varepsilon})^{\ast}} \mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i} k (y - x)} \zeta^{- \gamma + d / 2 - 1} \mathrm{e}^{- \zeta (k^2 + 1)} \varphi_{\delta} (\varepsilon | k |) \mathrm{d} \zeta, \end{array} \) (3.25)

for \(y, x \in (- L / 2, L / 2]^d\) and \(G_t^{L, \varepsilon} (y ; x) = 0\) otherwise, where the sum should be intended as integral if \(L = \infty\).

Remark 3.17. Note the dependence of \(G_t^{L, \varepsilon}\) on \(\delta\) when \(\varepsilon > 0\). We avoid writing this dependence explicitly to keep our notation as light as possible.

We abridge \(G^{\varepsilon}_t := G^{\infty, \varepsilon}_t\) and note that, because of translation invariance, the Poisson summation formula implies \(G_t^{L, \varepsilon} (y ; x) = \sum_{m \in \mathbb{Z}^d} G_t^{\varepsilon} (y + mL ; x)\), for \(y, x \in (- L / 2, L / 2]^d\). We also have

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{l} \dot{G}_t^{L, \varepsilon} (y ; x) = \boldsymbol{1}_{0 \leqslant t < 1} G_1^{L, \varepsilon} (y ; x)\\ \hspace{4em} + c_{d, \gamma} \chi_{1, - \log_2 \varepsilon} (t) U \frac{2^{(2 \gamma - d) s}}{L^d} \sum_{k \in \frac{2 \pi}{L} \mathbb{Z}^d} \mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i} k (y - x)} \mathrm{e}^{- 2^{- 2 t} (k^2 + 1)} \varphi (\varepsilon | k |) \end{array} \) (3.26)

where \(\chi_{a, b} (t)\) is a positive smooth approximation to \(\boldsymbol{1}_{a \leqslant t \leqslant b}\), such that \(\chi_{a, b} (t) = 1\) for \(a \leqslant t \leqslant b\) and \(\chi_{a, b} (t) = 0\) for \(t < a\) or \(t > b + \eta\) for some small fixed \(\eta > 0\) and where \(c_{d, \gamma}\) is some constant depending on \(d\) and \(\gamma\). We can write \(\dot{G}_t^{L {,} \varepsilon} = (\mathfrak{C}_t^{L, \varepsilon})^2 U\) and read off the explicit expression for \(\mathfrak{C}_t^{L, \varepsilon}\) from (3.26). Note the commutation relations \([\mathfrak{C}_t^{L, \varepsilon}, U] = [\mathfrak{C}_t^{L, \varepsilon}, \Theta] = 0\). We can finally introduce the following \((L, \varepsilon)\)-dependent family of GBMs.

Definition 3.18. We let \((X^{L, \varepsilon}_t)_t^{L \in \mathbb{N}_{\infty}, \varepsilon \in 2^{-\mathbb{N}_{\infty}}}\) be the anticommuting family of norm-compatible GBMs, see Definitions 2.15 and 2.18, such that

\(\displaystyle \omega (X_t^{L, \varepsilon} (f) X_t^{L', \varepsilon'} (g)) = \left\llangle f, \int_0^t \mathfrak{C}^{L, \varepsilon}_s \mathfrak{C}^{L', \varepsilon'}_s U \mathrm{d} s g \right\rrangle_{\Theta} \quad \forall f, g \in \mathfrak{h} .\)

Remark 3.19. In Appendix A, we prove that there exists a sufficiently large FNPS \((\mathcal{M}, \omega, (\mathcal{M}_t)_t)\) where such a family can be constructed. Note that we are requiring that \((X^{L, \varepsilon}_t)_t\) at different \(L\) and \(\varepsilon\) are correlated and this is actually natural if they originate from colouring the same “Grassmann white noise”, see Remark A.10. In particular, note that the Gaussian field \(X_t^{L, \varepsilon} - X_t^{L', \varepsilon'}\) has covariance \(\int_0^t (\mathfrak{C}^{L, \varepsilon}_s - \mathfrak{C}^{L', \varepsilon'}_s)^{\ast} (\mathfrak{C}^{L, \varepsilon}_s - \mathfrak{C}^{L', \varepsilon'}_s) U \mathrm{d} s\).

Even though \(X^{L, \varepsilon}_t (f)\) can be defined for \(f \in \mathfrak{h}\), in view of the application to the stochastic quantisation theorem, see Theorem 3.12, we think of them as indexed by \(\mathfrak{h}_{L, \varepsilon} \subset \mathfrak{h}\), when \(L \in \mathbb{N}\) and \(\varepsilon > 0\). In other words, for fixed \(L \in \mathbb{N}\) and \(\varepsilon > 0\), we consider only the image of \(\bigwedge \mathfrak{h}_{L, \varepsilon}\) under \(X_t^{L, \varepsilon}\). In this regard, it is particularly convenient to switch to the Kronecker delta basis and therefore obtain what we call a field on \(\mathbb{T}_{L, \varepsilon}^d\), that is, a map of the form \(\psi : \mathbb{T}^d_{L, \varepsilon} \rightarrow \mathcal{M}^4\).

Define \(\delta^{L, \varepsilon}_{x ; \uparrow} := (\delta^{L, \varepsilon}_x, 0) \in L^2 (\mathbb{T}^d_L ; \mathbb{C}^2)\) and \(\delta^{L, \varepsilon}_{x ; \downarrow} := (0, \delta_x^{L, \varepsilon}) \in L^2 (\mathbb{T}^d_L ; \mathbb{C}^2)\), where \(\delta_x^{L, \varepsilon}\) is the extension of the Kronecker delta to \(\mathbb{T}^d_L\) via Fourier transform, i.e. \(\delta_x^{L, \varepsilon} := \mathcal{F}_{L, 0}^{- 1} \mathcal{F}_{L, \varepsilon} \delta_x\). The set \((\delta^{L, \varepsilon}_{x ; \sigma})_{x \in \mathbb{T}^d_{L, \varepsilon}, \sigma = \uparrow, \downarrow}\) is an orthogonal basis for \(L^2 (\mathbb{T}^d_{L, \varepsilon} ; \mathbb{C}^2) \hookrightarrow L^2 (\mathbb{T}^d_L ; \mathbb{C}^2)\) (it is not orthonormal though).

Definition 3.20. If \((X^{L, \varepsilon}_t)_t^{L \in \mathbb{N}_{\infty}, \varepsilon \in 2^{-\mathbb{N}_{\infty}}}\) are the GBMs of Definition 3.18, by abuse of notation we let \(X^{L, \varepsilon}_t : \mathbb{R}^d \rightarrow \mathcal{M}^4\) denote the corresponding field on \(\mathbb{R}^d\), \(x \mapsto X_{t, x}^{L, \varepsilon} := \left( {X^{L, \varepsilon}_{t, x, \mu}}^{} \right)_{\mu \in \{ \uparrow, \downarrow \} \times \{ \pm \}}\) where

\(\displaystyle X_{t, x, (\sigma, +)}^{L, \varepsilon} := X_t^{L, \varepsilon} (\delta_{x ; \sigma}^{L, \varepsilon} \oplus 0), \qquad X_{t, x, (\sigma, -)}^{L, \varepsilon} := X_t^{L, \varepsilon} (0 \oplus \delta_{x ; \sigma}^{L, \varepsilon}), \) (3.27)

for \(x \in \mathbb{T}^d_L\) and \(X^{L, \varepsilon}_{t, x + m L} := X^{L, \varepsilon}_{t, x}\) for any \(m \in \mathbb{Z}^d\).

We introduce the following point-wise symmetric products for Grassmann fields. If \(\psi^{(1)}, \psi^{(2)}\) and \(\psi^{(3)}\) are fields we set:

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rll} (\psi^{(1)} \cdot \psi^{(2)})_x & := & \frac{1}{2} \sum_{\sigma = \uparrow, \downarrow} {\sum_{\pi \in S_2}}_{} {\psi_{x, (\sigma, +)}^{(\pi (1))}}^{} \psi^{(\pi (2))}_{x, (\sigma, -)},\\ (\psi^{(1)} \cdot \psi^{(2)} \cdot \psi^{(3)})_{x, \mu} & := & - \frac{1}{6} \operatorname{sign} (\mu) \sum_{\pi \in S_3} \psi^{(\pi (1))}_{x, \mu} (\psi^{(\pi (2))} \cdot \psi^{(\pi (3))})_x, \end{array} \)

where \(S_n\) denotes the set of permutations of \(n\) elements, and where if \(\mu = (\sigma, \rho) \in \{ \uparrow, \downarrow \} \times \{ \pm \}\) we set

\(\displaystyle \operatorname{sign} (\mu) := \rho . \) (3.28)

We also abridge the notation by setting \((\psi_x)^2 := (\psi \cdot \psi)_x\) and \((\psi_{x, \mu})^3 := (\psi \cdot \psi \cdot \psi)_{x, \mu}\).

We are finally in a position to apply the FBSDE to the construction of the interacting measure. In the FNPS \((\mathcal{M}, \omega, (\mathcal{M}_t)_t)\) we are interested in computing the expectation of observables

3.2. See also Lemma 4.29 for the actual applications.

3.2 of the form \(P (\psi)\), for some \(P \in \bigwedge B^{\gamma^+}_{1, 1, 0^-}\), see (1.12) and Remark 2.2 , so that \(P (\psi) \in \mathcal{A}\). This expectation is obtained as the weak limit

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} \omega^V (P (\psi)) & = & \lim_{L \rightarrow \infty} \lim_{\varepsilon \rightarrow 0} \frac{\omega (P (X^{L, \varepsilon}) \mathrm{e}^{V_{\infty}^{L, \varepsilon} (X^{L, \varepsilon})})}{\omega (\mathrm{e}^{V_{\infty}^{L, \varepsilon} (X^{L, \varepsilon})})}\\ & = & \lim_{L \rightarrow \infty} \lim_{\varepsilon \rightarrow 0} \omega (P (\Psi^{L, \varepsilon}))^{}\\ & = & \omega (P (\Psi)), \end{array}\)

with \(X^{L, \varepsilon} := X^{L, \varepsilon}_{\infty}\), \(\Psi^{L, \varepsilon} := \Psi^{L, \varepsilon}_{\infty}\), \(P (\Psi) = \lim_{L \rightarrow \infty} \lim_{\varepsilon \rightarrow 0} P (\Psi^{L, \varepsilon})\) in \(\mathcal{M}\) (see Remark 4.26), where \(X^{L, \varepsilon}_t\) are the GBMs introduced above, where the potential \(V_T^{L, \varepsilon}\) reads

\(\displaystyle V_T^{L, \varepsilon} (X^{L, \varepsilon}) := \int_{\mathbb{T}^d_{L, \varepsilon}} \left[ \frac{\lambda}{2} [(X_x^{L, \varepsilon})^2]^2 +^{} \mu^{\varepsilon}_T (X_x^{L, \varepsilon})^2 \right] \mathrm{d} x, \) (3.29)

for suitable constant \(^{} \mu^{\varepsilon}_T = \mu_T^{\varepsilon} (\lambda) \in \mathbb{R}\), and where the lattice field \(\Psi^{L, \varepsilon}_t\) solves for \(T = \infty\)

\(\displaystyle \mathrm{d} \Psi^{L, \varepsilon}_t = - \dot{G}_t^{L, \varepsilon} \omega_t (\mathrm{D} V_T^{L, \varepsilon} (\Psi^{L, \varepsilon}_T)) \mathrm{d} t + \mathrm{d} X^{L, \varepsilon}_t , \hspace{3em} \Psi^{L, \varepsilon}_0 = 0.\) (3.30)

Note that by (3.27), the drift term is

\(\displaystyle \mathrm{D} V_T^{L, \varepsilon} (\Psi^{L, \varepsilon}_T) (y ; \mu) = \lambda (\Psi^{L, \varepsilon}_{T, y, \mu})^3 -\operatorname{sign} (\mu) \mu^{\varepsilon}_T \Psi^{L, \varepsilon}_{T, y, - \mu} . \)

Remark 3.21. Let \(\tilde{G}_t^{L, \varepsilon}\) denote the periodisation of \(G_t^{L, \varepsilon}\), so that \(\tilde{G}_t^{L, \varepsilon} (y + mL ; x + m' L) = \tilde{G}_t^{L, \varepsilon} (y ; x)\) for any \(m, m' \in \mathbb{Z}^d\). Then, we can equivalently solve

\(\displaystyle \Psi^{L, \varepsilon}_{t, x, \mu} = - \sum_{\mu'} \int_0^t \int_{\mathbb{T}^d_L} (\dot{\tilde{G}}^{L, \varepsilon}_s (x ; y))_{\mu, \mu'} \omega_s (\mathrm{D} V_T^{\varepsilon} (\Psi^{L, \varepsilon}_T) (y ; - \mu')) \mathrm{d} y \mathrm{d} s + X^{L, \varepsilon}_{t, x, \mu}, \) (3.31)

where \(V^{\varepsilon}_T \equiv V_T^{\infty, \varepsilon}\) is as in (3.29) but with integral over the whole \(\mathbb{R}^d\). This defines an extension of the solution to the whole \(\mathbb{R}^d\), satisfying \(\Psi^{L, \varepsilon}_{t, x + m L} = \Psi^{L, \varepsilon}_{t, x}\) for any \(m \in \mathbb{Z}^d\).

Using Lemma 3.14, we solve the FBSDE by means of an interpolation scheme, for suitable \(F^{L, \varepsilon}_t\). As it turns out, we can actually take it already in the infinite-volume case and likewise replace \(\dot{\tilde{G}}^{L, \varepsilon}_t\) by \(\dot{G}^{\varepsilon}_t\) in (3.31): in fact, because of translation invariance and because the field \(X^{L, \varepsilon}_t\) is periodic, so is the solution \(\Psi^{L, \varepsilon}_t\) and \(F^{\varepsilon}_t (\Psi^{L, \varepsilon}_t)\). We make this precise in the following lemma, which will be proven in Section 4.

Lemma 3.22. Let \((\Psi^{L, \varepsilon}_t, R^{L, \varepsilon}_t)\) solve the system

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rll} \Psi^{L, \varepsilon}_t & = & - \int_0^t \dot{G}^{\varepsilon}_s (F^{\varepsilon}_s (\Psi^{L, \varepsilon}_s) + R^{L, \varepsilon}_s) \mathrm{d} s + X^{L, \varepsilon}_t,\\ R^{L, \varepsilon}_t & = & \int_t^{\infty} \omega_t (\mathcal{H}_s [F^{\varepsilon}_s] (\Psi_s)) \mathrm{d} s + \int_t^{\infty} \omega_t (\mathrm{D} F^{\varepsilon}_s (\Psi^{L, \varepsilon}_s) \cdot \dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon} R^{L, \varepsilon}_s) \mathrm{d} s \end{array}\)

with \(\mathcal{H}_s [F^{\varepsilon}_s] := \partial_s F^{\varepsilon}_s + \frac{1}{2} \mathrm{D}_{\dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon}}^2 F^{\varepsilon}_s + \mathrm{D} F^{\varepsilon}_s \cdot \dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon} F^{\varepsilon}_s\). Then, \(\Psi^{L, \varepsilon}_t\) is periodic and solves the FBSDE (3.30).

3.4Sobolev spaces and covariance estimates

Before delving into the details of the solution, we conclude the section by introducing suitable norms on fields on \(\mathbb{R}^d\)

3.3. If fields are on the torus \(\mathbb{T}_L^d\), the definitions below carry over by simply identifying \(\mathbb{T}^d_L\) with the box \((- L / 2, L / 2]^d \subset \mathbb{R}^d\).

3.3 . The crucial property of (regularised) Grassmann field is that they are (smooth) bounded objects, unlike standard (commutative) Gaussian random variables. This motivates working with the space \(L^{\infty} (\mathbb{R}^d ; \mathcal{M}^4)\), which is introduced in the usual way as the \(L^{\infty}\) space of functions taking values in a Banach space. This space is equipped with the norm

\(\displaystyle \| \varphi \|_{L^{\infty} (\mathbb{R}^d ; \mathcal{M}^4)} := \sup_{\mu \in \{ \uparrow, \downarrow \} \times \{ \pm \}} ^{} \sup_{x \in \mathbb{R}^d} \| \varphi_{x, \mu} \| .\)

We will also need to control the size of the derivatives of Grassmann fields. If \(n \in \mathbb{N}_0\) and \(\varphi : \mathbb{R}^d \rightarrow \mathcal{M}^4\) is sufficiently smooth, we introduce the notation

\(\displaystyle \partial^0 \varphi := \varphi \qquad \partial^n \varphi := \partial^n \varphi := (\partial_{\nu}^n \varphi)_{\nu \in \{ 1, \ldots, d \}^n},\)

to denote the tensor of the fields with \(n\) derivatives, where if \(\nu = (\nu_i)_{i = 1}^n \in \{ 1, \ldots, d \}^n\)

\(\displaystyle \partial^n_{\nu} \varphi (x) := \frac{\partial^n}{\partial^{\nu} x} \varphi (x) = \frac{\partial^n \varphi (x)}{\partial x_{v_1} \cdots \partial x_{v_n}} .\)

We introduce the following weighted Sobolev spaces

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{C}^n_t (\mathbb{R}^d ; \mathcal{M}^4) := \{\varphi : \mathbb{R}^d \rightarrow \mathcal{M}^4 | \hspace{0.8em} \| \varphi \|_{\mathcal{C}^n_t (\mathbb{R}^d ; \mathcal{M}^4)} < \infty\}\)

where

\(\displaystyle \| \varphi \|_{\mathcal{C}^n_t (\mathbb{R}^d ; \mathcal{M}^4)} := {\sum_{\nu \in \{ 1, \ldots, d \}^n}}_{} 2^{- n t} \| \partial_{\nu}^n \varphi \|_{L^{\infty} (\mathbb{R}^d ; \mathcal{M}^4)} . \) (3.32)

Note that the seminorms (3.32) controls only the size of the derivatives and is therefore a norm only after quotienting, which we left implicit in the definition of \(\mathcal{C}^n_t (\mathbb{R}^d ; \mathcal{M}^4)\). For simplicity, we abridge the notation to \(\| \cdot \|_{L^{\infty}}\), \(\| \cdot \|_{\mathcal{C}^n_t}\). For controlling the infinite-volume limit, we shall also consider the following weighted seminorms

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} \| \varphi \|_{L^{\infty} (\eta)} & := & \sup_{\mu \in \{ \uparrow, \downarrow \} \times \{ \pm \}} ^{} \sup_{x \in \mathbb{R}^d} \varrho_{\eta} (x) \| \varphi_{x, \mu} \|,\\ \| \varphi \|_{\mathcal{C}^n_t (\eta)} & := & {\sum^{}_{\nu \in \{ 1, \ldots, d \}^n}}_{} 2^{- n t} \| \partial_{\nu}^n \varphi \|_{L^{\infty} (\eta)}, \end{array} \) (3.33)

where \(\varrho_{\eta} (x) := (1 + | x |^2)^{- \eta / 2}\) satisfies the compatibility condition \(\varrho_{\eta} (x) \lesssim_{\eta} \frac{\varrho_{\eta} (y)}{\varrho_{\eta} (y - x)}\), as follows straightforwardly by the triangular inequality.

In Section 4.1, we study multilinear operators in the fields. To this end, we also consider fields on \(\mathbb{R}^{dk}\) for some \(k \in \mathbb{N}\), as maps \(\varphi : \mathbb{R}^{dk} \rightarrow \mathcal{M}^{4^k}\), since in fact, if \(\varphi\) is a field on \(\mathbb{R}^2\), the tensor product \(\psi^{\otimes k}\) is a field on \(\mathbb{R}^{dk}\). Given \(\varphi : \mathbb{R}^{2 q} \rightarrow \mathcal{M}^{4^k}\) and a tuple \(m = (m_i)_{i = 1}^k\), we let \(\partial^m \psi := (\partial^{m_1} \otimes \cdots \otimes \partial^{m_q}) \psi\). For the sake of brevity, we abridge our notation to

\(\displaystyle \psi^{(m)} := \partial^m \psi .\)

If \(\psi\) is a field on \(\mathbb{R}^2\) and \(m = (m_i)_{i = 1}^k\) is a tuple, we will further abridge our notation to

\(\displaystyle \psi^{\otimes (m)} := (\psi^{\otimes k})^{(m)} = (\partial^{m_1} \psi) \otimes \cdots \otimes (\partial^{m_k} \psi) .\)

On fields on \(\mathbb{R}^{dk}\), we consider the seminorms

\(\displaystyle \| \varphi \|_{\mathcal{C}^m_t (\mathbb{R}^{dk} ; \mathcal{M}^{4^k})} := \sum_{\text{\scriptsize{$\begin{array}{c} \nu_1, \ldots, \nu_k\\ | \nu_i | = m_i \end{array}$}}} 2^{- [m] t} \| (\partial^{m_1} \otimes \cdots \otimes \partial^{m_k}) \varphi \|_{L^{\infty} (\mathbb{R}^{dk} ; \mathcal{M}^{4^k})} . \) (3.34)

where \([m] := \sum_i m_i\), and let

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{C}^m_t (\mathbb{R}^{dk} ; \mathcal{M}^{4^k}) := \left\{ \varphi : \mathbb{R}^{dk} \rightarrow \mathcal{M}^{4^k} | \| \varphi \|_{\mathcal{C}^m_t (\mathbb{R}^{dk} ; \mathcal{M}^{4^k})} < \infty \right\},\)

where the quotienting is again implicit. We conclude this section with some estimates on the covariance and on the GBM of Definition 3.18.

Lemma 3.23. Let \(\varepsilon \in 2^{-\mathbb{N}_{\infty}}\) and let \(G_t^{\varepsilon}\) be as in Definition 3.16. Then,

\(\displaystyle \sup_{\mu, \mu'} \sup_{\nu \in \{ 1, \ldots, d \}^n} \left| \frac{\partial^n}{\partial^{\nu} x} (\dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon} (x ; y))^{}_{\mu, \mu'} \right| \lesssim_n 2^{(2 \gamma + n) s} \mathrm{e}^{- \bar{c} (2^s | x - y |)^{\delta}} ,\) (3.35)

for some universal \(\bar{c}\), with \(\delta = 1\) if \(\varepsilon = 0\) and otherwise as in Definition 3.16. If \(\varepsilon > \varepsilon' \in 2^{-\mathbb{N}_{\infty}}\) and \(\theta > 0\), then

\(\displaystyle \sup_{\mu, \mu'} \sup_{\nu \in \{ 1, \ldots, d \}^n} \left| \frac{\partial^n}{\partial^{\nu} x} (\dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon} (x ; y) - \dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon'} (x ; y))^{}_{\mu, \mu'} \right| \lesssim^{}_{n, \theta} \varepsilon^{\theta} 2^{(2 \gamma + n + \theta) s} \mathrm{e}^{- \bar{c} (2^s | x - y |)^{\delta}} .\) (3.36)

Because of the translational invariance of \(\dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon}\), we can consider it to be a \(\mathbb{C}^{4 \times 4}\)-valued function on \(\mathbb{R}^d\). We let \(L^p_{s ; c} (\mathbb{R}^d ; \mathbb{C}^{4 \times 4})\) be the \(L^p\) space restricted on \(\mathbb{R}^d\) with respect to the measure \(w_{s ; c} (x) \mathrm{d} x\), where

\(\displaystyle w_{s ; c} (x) := \mathrm{e}^{c (2^s | x |)^{\delta}} \) (3.37)

for \(c \geqslant 0\) and with \(\delta\) as in Definition 3.16. The following corollary is a simple consequence of Lemma 3.23.

Corollary 3.24. Under the same assumptions of Lemma 3.23, we have

\(\displaystyle \sup_{\nu \in \{ 1, \ldots, d \}^n} \| \partial^n_{\nu} \dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon} \|_{L^p_{s ; c}} \lesssim_n 2^{(2 \gamma + n - d / p) s}, \) (3.38)

\(\displaystyle \sup_{\nu \in \{ 1, \ldots, d \}^n} \| \partial^n_{\nu} (\dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon} - \dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon'}) \|_{L^p_{s ; c}} \lesssim_{n, \theta} 2^{(2 \gamma + n + \theta - d / p) s} \varepsilon^{\theta},\)

for \(c < \bar{c}\), where \(\lesssim\) depends on the weight as well.

Proof of Lemma 3.23. We focus on the case \(s \in [1, - \log_2 \varepsilon]\), since for \(s \in [0, 1)\) the proof is similar. We note that \((\partial^n_{\nu} \dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon})_{\mu, \mu'}\) is proportional to the integral (up to universal factors)

\(\displaystyle \left( \frac{\partial^n}{\partial^{\nu} x} \dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon} \right)_{\mu, \mu'} (y ; x) \sim 2^{(2 \gamma + n) s} \int_{\mathbb{R}^d} \mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i} k 2^s (y - x)} \mathrm{e}^{- (k^2 + 2^{- 2 s})} \left[ \prod^n_{i = 1} - \mathrm{i} k_{\nu_i} \right] \varphi_{\delta} (2^s \varepsilon | k |) \mathrm{d} k. \) (3.39)

We want to prove a bound of the form

\(\displaystyle \left| 2^{ms} | y - x |^m (\partial^n_{\nu} \dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon})_{\mu, \mu'} (y ; x)^{} \right| \lesssim_n C^m (m!)^{\delta^{- 1}} \qquad \forall m \in \mathbb{N} \) (3.40)

from which (3.35) for \(\varepsilon > 0\) follows by optimising over \(m \in \mathbb{N}\). To prove (3.40), we use integration by parts on (3.39)

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{l} \left| 2^{m s} | y - x |^m (\partial^n_{\nu} \dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon})_{\mu, \mu'} (y ; x)^{} \right|\\ \qquad \lesssim 2^{(2 \gamma + n) s} \tilde{C}^n \sup_{\nu'} \int_{\mathbb{R}^d} \left| \frac{\partial^m}{\partial^{\nu'} k} \mathrm{e}^{- (k^2 + 2^{- 2 s})} \left[ \prod^n_{i = 1} - \mathrm{i} k_{\nu_i} \right] \varphi_{\delta} (2^s \varepsilon | k |) \right| \mathrm{d} k. \end{array}\)

By using the Gevrey condition, we have

\(\displaystyle \sup_{k \in \mathbb{R}^d} \left| \frac{\partial^m}{\partial^{\nu'} k} \varphi_{\delta} (2^s \varepsilon | k |) \right| \leqslant C^{1 + m} (2^s \varepsilon)^m (m!)^{\delta^{- 1}}\)

whereas, by using the analyticity in a strip of size one around the real axis we have \(\left| \frac{\partial^n}{\partial^{\nu'} k} \mathrm{e}^{- (k^2 + 2^{- 2 s})} \right| \leqslant C^{1 + m} {m! \mathrm{e}^{- k^2}}^{}\). Since \(2^s \varepsilon \leqslant 1\), (3.40) follows.

Repeating the same strategy for the difference, we obtain

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{l} \left| 2^{ms} | y - x |^m \left[ \left( \frac{\partial^n}{\partial^{\nu} x} \dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon} \right)_{\mu, \mu'} (y ; x) - \left( \frac{\partial^n}{\partial^{\nu} x} \dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon'} \right)_{\mu, \mu'} (y ; x) \right] \right|\\ \qquad \lesssim_n 2^{(2 \gamma + n) s} \tilde{C}^n \sup_{\nu'} \int_{\mathbb{R}^d}^{} \left| \frac{\partial^m}{\partial^{\nu'} k} \mathrm{e}^{- (k^2 + 2^{- 2 s})} \left[ \prod^n_{i = 1} - \mathrm{i} k_{\nu_i} \right] (\varphi_{\delta} (2^s \varepsilon | k |) - \varphi_{\delta} (2^s \varepsilon' | k |)) \right| \mathrm{d} k. \end{array} \) (3.41)

We note that \(\varphi_{\delta} (2^s \varepsilon | k |) - \varphi_{\delta} (2^s \varepsilon' | k |)\) and its derivatives are vanishing unless \(| k | \gtrsim \varepsilon^{- 1} 2^{- s}\), therefore

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{l} \left| 2^{m s} | y - x |^m \left[ \left( \frac{\partial^n}{\partial^{\nu} x} \dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon} \right)_{\mu, \mu'} (y ; x) - \left( \frac{\partial^n}{\partial^{\nu} x} \dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon'} \right)_{\mu, \mu'} (y ; x) \right] \right|\\ \quad \lesssim_n 2^{(2 \gamma + n) s} C^m (m!)^{\delta^{- 1}} \mathrm{e}^{- \frac{(\varepsilon^{} 2^s)^{- 2}}{2}}\\ \quad \leqslant 2^{(2 \gamma + n + \theta) s} C^m (m!)^{\delta^{- 1}} \sup_{s \in \mathbb{R}^+} 2^{- \theta s} \mathrm{e}^{- \frac{(\varepsilon^{} 2^s)^{- 2}}{2}}\\ \quad \lesssim_{\theta} 2^{(2 \gamma + n + \theta) s} \varepsilon^{\theta} C^m (m!)^{\delta^{- 1}}, \end{array}\)

so that, optimising over \(m \in \mathbb{N}\) gives the stretched exponential decay in (3.36).

When \(\varepsilon = 0\), we have \(\varphi_{\delta} (2^s \varepsilon | k |) \equiv 1\) and thus the integrand is analytic in a strip around the real axis in each of the variables \((k_i)_{i = 1}^d\). Accordingly, we deform the integration contour by shifting \(k_1 \rightarrow k_1 + \mathrm{i} \operatorname{sign} (y_1 - x_1) / 2\) and obtain

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{ll} \left| \left( \frac{\partial^n}{\partial^{\nu} x} \dot{G}_s \right)_{\mu, \mu'} (2^{- s} y ; 2^{- s} x) \right| & \lesssim \: 2^{(2 \gamma + n) s} \mathrm{e}^{- | y_1 - x_1 | / 2} \int_{\mathbb{R}^d} \mathrm{e}^{-\operatorname{Re} \left( k^2 - \frac{1}{2} + \mathrm{i} k_1 \operatorname{sign} (y_1 - x_1) \right)} | k |^n \mathrm{d} k\\ & \lesssim_n \: 2^{(2 \gamma + n) s} \mathrm{e}^{- | y_1 - x_1 | / 2} . \end{array}\)

By rotation invariance this implies the bound (3.35).\(\Box\)

Remark 3.25. In a similar way and by using the Poisson summation formula to restrict to finite \(L\), one can prove a similar decay estimate for \(\mathfrak{C}_s^{L, \varepsilon}\), that is,

\(\displaystyle \sup_{\mu, \mu'} \sup_{\nu \in \{ 1, \ldots, d \}^n} \left| \frac{\partial^n}{\partial^{\nu} x} (\mathfrak{C}^{L, \varepsilon}_s (x ; y))^{}_{\mu, \mu'} \right| \lesssim 2^{(\gamma + d / 2 + n) s} \sum_{m \in \mathbb{Z}^d} \mathrm{e}^{- \bar{c} (2^s | x - y - m L |)^{\delta}},\)

with \(\delta = 1\) if \(\varepsilon = 0\), and for the difference \(\frac{\partial^n}{\partial^{\nu} x} (\mathfrak{C}_s^{L, \varepsilon} (x ; y) - \mathfrak{C}_s^{L, \varepsilon'} (x ; y))^{}_{\mu, \mu'}\). Note also the bounds

\(\displaystyle \sup_{\nu \in \{ 1, \ldots, d \}^n} \| \partial^n_{\nu} \mathfrak{C}_s^{L, \varepsilon} \|_{L^p_{s ; c} (\mathbb{T}^d_L ; \mathbb{C}^{4 \times 4})} \lesssim_{n, c} 2^{(\gamma + d / 2 + n - d / p) s} . \) (3.42)

We also have the following estimates.

Corollary 3.26. Let \(X^{L, \varepsilon}_t\) be the field in Definition 3.20. Then, for any \(L' \geqslant L \in \mathbb{N}_{\infty}\), \(\varepsilon \geqslant \varepsilon' \in 2^{-\mathbb{N}_{\infty}}\), \(\theta \geqslant 0\) and \(\eta \geqslant 0\)

\(\displaystyle \| X^{L, \varepsilon}_t \|_{\mathcal{C}^n_t} \lesssim_n 2^{\gamma t}, \hspace{3em} \| X^{L, \varepsilon}_t - X_t^{L', \varepsilon'} \|_{\mathcal{C}^n_t (\eta)} \lesssim_{n, \theta, \eta} 2^{\gamma t} [2^{\theta t} \varepsilon^{\theta} \boldsymbol{1}_{\varepsilon > \varepsilon'} + L^{- \eta} \boldsymbol{1}_{L' > L}] .\)

Furthermore, for any \(L' \geqslant L \in \mathbb{N}_{\infty}\), \(\varepsilon \geqslant \varepsilon' \in 2^{-\mathbb{N}_{\infty}}\), \(\theta \geqslant 0\), \(\eta \geqslant 0\) and \(\rho > 0\), uniformly in \(t \in [0, \infty]\),

\(\displaystyle \| X_t^{L, \varepsilon} \|_{B^{- \gamma - \rho}_{\infty, \infty}} \lesssim \rho^{- 1 / 2}, \qquad \| X^{L, \varepsilon}_t - X^{L', \varepsilon'}_t \|_{B^{- \gamma - \theta - \rho}_{\infty, \infty, \eta}} \lesssim \rho^{- 1 / 2} (\varepsilon^{\theta} \boldsymbol{1}_{\varepsilon > \varepsilon'} + L^{- \eta} \boldsymbol{1}_{L' > L}) . \) (3.43)

Proof. The proof is a consequence of the norm compatibility and the exponential estimates for \(\mathfrak{C}_t^{L, \varepsilon}\), \(\mathfrak{C}_t^{L, \varepsilon} - \mathfrak{C}_t^{L, \varepsilon'}\), see Remarks 3.25, and \(\mathfrak{C}_t^{\varepsilon} - \mathfrak{C}_t^{L, \varepsilon}\). When we consider differences of fields, we shall use

\(\displaystyle \| X^{L, \varepsilon}_t (f) - X_t^{L', \varepsilon'} (f') \| \leqslant \| X^{L, \varepsilon}_t (f) - X^{L', \varepsilon'}_t (f) \| + \| X^{L', \varepsilon'}_t (f - f') \|,\)

with, e.g., \(f = \delta^{L, \varepsilon}_x \oplus 0 \in \mathfrak{h}_L \cong L^2 (\mathbb{T}^d_L ; \mathbb{C}^4)^{}\) and \(f' = \delta^{L', \varepsilon'}_x \oplus 0 \in \mathfrak{h}_{L'}\) or their derivatives, see Definition 3.20. Accordingly, we have that

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} \| \partial^n_{\nu} X^{L, \varepsilon}_{t, x} - \partial^n_{\nu} X_{t, x}^{L, \varepsilon'} \| & \lesssim & \left( \int_0^t \| (\mathfrak{C}_s^{L, \varepsilon} - \mathfrak{C}_s^{L, \varepsilon'}) \partial^n_{\nu} \delta^{L, \varepsilon}_x \|^2_{\mathfrak{h}_L} \mathrm{d} s \right)^{1 / 2}\\ & & \qquad + \left( \int_0^t \| \mathfrak{C}_s^{L, \varepsilon'} \partial^n_{\nu} (\delta^{L, \varepsilon}_x - \delta^{L, \varepsilon'}_x) \|_{\mathfrak{h}_L}^2 \mathrm{d} s \right)^{1 / 2}\\ & \lesssim_{n, \theta} & 2^{(\gamma + n + \theta) t} \varepsilon^{\theta}, \end{array}\)

where in the first term we used that \(| \varphi_{\delta} (\varepsilon | k |) - \varphi_{\delta} (\varepsilon^{- 1} | k |) | \lesssim (| k | \varepsilon)^{\theta} | \varphi_{\delta} (\varepsilon | k |) - \varphi_{\delta} (\varepsilon^{- 1} | k |) |\), compare with the definition of \(\mathfrak{C}_s^{L, \varepsilon}\), whereas in the second that the Fourier series of \(\delta^{L, \varepsilon}_x - \delta^{L, \varepsilon'}_x\) starts from \(\gtrsim \varepsilon^{- 1}\) and that \(\boldsymbol{1}_{| k | \gtrsim \varepsilon^{- 1}} \lesssim \boldsymbol{1}_{| k | \gtrsim \varepsilon^{- 1}} (| k | \varepsilon)^{\theta}\). This proves the bound on \(\| X^{L, \varepsilon}_t - X_t^{L, \varepsilon'} \|_{\mathcal{C}^n_t}\) . The bound on \(\| X^{L, \varepsilon}_t \|_{\mathcal{C}^n_t}\) follows in an easier way.

The claim on \(\| X^{L,' \varepsilon}_t - X_t^{L, \varepsilon} \|_{\mathcal{C}^n_t (\eta)}\), follows by proving the same estimate for \(\| X^{\varepsilon}_t - X_t^{L, \varepsilon} \|_{\mathcal{C}^n_t (\eta)}\). We have

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} \| \partial^n_{\nu} X^{\varepsilon}_{t, x} - \partial^n_{\nu} X_{t, x}^{L, \varepsilon} \| & \lesssim & \left( \int_0^t \| (\mathfrak{C}_s^{\varepsilon} - \mathfrak{C}_s^{L, \varepsilon}) \partial^n_{\nu} \delta^{\varepsilon}_x \|^2_{\mathfrak{h}} \mathrm{d} s \right)^{1 / 2}\\ \hspace{10em} & & \quad + \left( \int_0^t \| \mathfrak{C}_s^{\varepsilon} \partial^n_{\nu} (\delta^{\varepsilon}_x - \delta^{L, \varepsilon}_x) \|_{\mathfrak{h}}^2 \mathrm{d} s \right)^{1 / 2}\\ & =: & a_x + b_x . \end{array}\)

where \(\mathfrak{h} \cong L^2 (\mathbb{R}^d ; \mathbb{C}^4)\). To control \(\sup_{x \in \mathbb{R}^d} \varrho_{\eta} (x) a_x\) we split the integration coming from \(\| \cdot \|^2_{\mathfrak{h}}\) as follows:

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} \sup_{x \in \mathbb{R}^d} \varrho_{\eta} (x) a_x & \lesssim & \sup_{x \in B_{L / 2}} \left( \int_0^t \int_{B_L} \frac{\varrho_{\eta} (z)^2}{\varrho_{\eta} (z - x)^2} \left| \frac{\partial^n}{\partial^{\nu} x} (\mathfrak{C}_s^{\varepsilon} - \mathfrak{C}_s^{L, \varepsilon}) (x ; z) \right|^2 \mathrm{d} z \mathrm{d} s \right)^{1 / 2}\\ & & + \sup_{x \in (B_{L / 2})^c} \varrho_{\eta} (x) \left( \int_0^t \int_{B_L} \left| \frac{\partial^n}{\partial^{\nu} x} (\mathfrak{C}_s^{\varepsilon} - \mathfrak{C}_s^{L, \varepsilon}) (x ; z) \right|^2 \mathrm{d} z \mathrm{d} s \right)^{1 / 2}\\ & & + \sup_{x \in \mathbb{R}^d} \left( \int_0^t \int_{(B_{L / 2})^c} \frac{\varrho_{\eta} (z)^2}{\varrho_{\eta} (z - x)^2} \left| \frac{\partial^n}{\partial^{\nu} x} \mathfrak{C}_s^{\varepsilon} (x ; z) \right|^2 \mathrm{d} z \mathrm{d} s \right)^{1 / 2}\\ & =: & \mathrm{I} + \mathrm{I} \mathrm{I} + \mathrm{I} \mathrm{I} \mathrm{I}, \end{array} \) (3.44)

where we used the property \(\varrho_{\eta} (x) \lesssim_{\eta} \varrho_{\eta} (z) / \varrho_{\eta} (z - x)\) and where \(B_L := [- L / 2, L / 2]^d\). We bound \(\mathrm{I}\) by noting that for \(x, z \in B_L\) \((\mathfrak{C}_s^{\varepsilon} - \mathfrak{C}_s^{L, \varepsilon}) (x ; z) = - \sum_{m \in \mathbb{Z}^d \setminus \{ 0 \}} \mathfrak{C}_s^{\varepsilon} (x ; z + m L)\) and by using the estimate for \(\partial^{\nu} \mathfrak{C}_s^{\varepsilon}\) for \(x \in B_{L / 2}\), which controls the growing term \(\varrho_{\eta}^{- 1}\). The fact that \(| x - z | \leqslant L / 2\) and the term \(m = 0\) is removed from the Poisson summation allows us to gain extra decay in \(L\). Hence, we have

\(\displaystyle \mathrm{I} \lesssim \left( \int_0^t 2^{(2 \gamma + 2 n) s} 2^{- \bar{c} (2^s L)^{\delta} / 2} \sum_{m, m' \in \mathbb{Z}^d} \mathrm{e}^{- \frac{| m | + | m' |}{2} L} \mathrm{d} s \right)^{1 / 2} \lesssim 2^{- \bar{c} L^{\delta} / 2} .\)

We bound \(\mathrm{I} \mathrm{I}\) by using the decay of \(\mathfrak{C}_s^{\varepsilon}\) and \(\mathfrak{C}_s^{L, \varepsilon}\) separately to integrate over \(B_L\), the decay in \(L\) being obtained directly from the weight \(\varrho_{\eta}\), since \(x\) is away from the origin; thus, we have \(\mathrm{I} \mathrm{I} \lesssim 2^{(\gamma + n) t} L^{- \eta}\). To bound \(\mathrm{I} \mathrm{I} \mathrm{I}\) we use \(\varrho_{\eta} (z) \lesssim L^{- \eta}\) and control the remaining integral using the decay of \(\mathfrak{C}_s^{\varepsilon}\), implying the same bound as for \(\mathrm{I}\) and \(\mathrm{I} \mathrm{I}\).

To control \(\sup_{x \in \mathbb{R}^d} \varrho_{\eta} (x) b_x\) we split the integral as in (3.44) and follow the same strategy as above: in particular, if \(z \in \mathbb{T}^d_L\) we use the Poisson summation formula \(\delta^{\varepsilon}_x (z) - \delta^{L, \varepsilon}_x (z) = - \sum_{m \in \mathbb{Z}^d \setminus \{ 0 \}} \delta_x^{\varepsilon} (z + m L)_{}\) to write \((\mathfrak{C}_s^{\varepsilon} \partial^n_{\nu} (\delta^{\varepsilon}_x - \delta^{L, \varepsilon}_x)) (z) = - \sum_{m \in \mathbb{Z}^d \setminus \{ 0 \}} \frac{\partial^n}{\partial^{\nu} x} \mathfrak{C}_s^{\varepsilon} (x + mL ; z)\), whereas for \(z \in (B_L)^c\) we simply have \((\mathfrak{C}_s^{\varepsilon} \partial^n_{\nu} (\delta^{\varepsilon}_x - \delta^{L, \varepsilon}_x)) (z) = \frac{\partial^n}{\partial^{\nu} x} \mathfrak{C}_s^{\varepsilon} (x ; z)\) and the extra decay in \(L\) is obtained from \(\varrho_{\eta}\). Therefore \(\sup_{x \in \mathbb{R}^d} \varrho_{\eta} (x) b_x \lesssim 2^{(\gamma + n) t} L^{- \eta}\) and thus the desired bound is proven.

We are left with proving (3.43). Denote by \((\Delta_j)_{j \geqslant - 1}\) the Littlewood–Paley blocks. We have

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} \| X^{L, \varepsilon}_t (\Delta_j (\cdot - x)) - X^{L, \varepsilon'}_t (\Delta_j (\cdot - x)) \| & \lesssim & \left( \int_0^t \| (\mathfrak{C}_s^{L, \varepsilon} - \mathfrak{C}_s^{L, \varepsilon'}) \boldsymbol{1}_{B_L} \Delta_j (\cdot - x) \|^2_{\mathfrak{h}_L} \mathrm{d} s \right)^{1 / 2}\\ & \lesssim & \left( \int_0^t 2^{- 2 \rho s} {\varepsilon^{2 \theta}}^{} \| (\mathbb{1} - \Delta)^{\gamma / 2 + \theta / 2 + \rho / 2 - d / 4} \Delta_j (\cdot - x) \|^2_{\mathfrak{h}} \mathrm{d} s \right)^{1 / 2}\\ & \lesssim & \rho^{- 1 / 2} {\varepsilon^{\theta}}^{} 2^{(\gamma + \theta + \rho) j}, \end{array}\)

\(\Delta\) being the Laplacian, where we used that \(| \varphi_{\delta} (\varepsilon | k |) - \varphi_{\delta} (\varepsilon^{- 1} | k |) |^2 \lesssim (| k | \varepsilon)^{2 \theta} | \varphi_{\delta} (\varepsilon | k |) - \varphi_{\delta} (\varepsilon^{- 1} | k |) |^2\) and extracted sufficient decay in \(s\) by taking \(\rho > 0\). The bound on \(\| X^{L, \varepsilon}_t (\Delta_j (\cdot - x)) \|\) follows likewise. Finally, we prove the last bound following a strategy similar to (3.44). We split \(\| \cdot \|^2_{\mathfrak{h}}\) as

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{l} \| (\mathfrak{C}_s^{\varepsilon} - \mathfrak{C}_s^{L, \varepsilon}) w^{- 1} w \Delta_j (\cdot - x) \|^2_{\mathfrak{h}}\\ \qquad \lesssim \| \boldsymbol{1}_{B_L} (\mathfrak{C}_s^{\varepsilon} - \mathfrak{C}_s^{L, \varepsilon}) w^{- 1} \boldsymbol{1}_{B_{L / 2}} w \Delta_j (\cdot - x) \|^2_{\mathfrak{h}} + \| \boldsymbol{1}_{(B_L)^c} \mathfrak{C}_s^{\varepsilon} w^{- 1} \boldsymbol{1}_{B_{L / 2}} w \Delta_j (\cdot - x) \|^2_{\mathfrak{h}}\\ \hspace{4em} + \| (\mathfrak{C}_s^{\varepsilon} - \mathfrak{C}_s^{L, \varepsilon}) w^{- 1} \boldsymbol{1}_{(B_{L / 2})^c} w \Delta_j (\cdot - x) \|^2_{\mathfrak{h}} =: \mathrm{I} + \mathrm{I} \mathrm{I} + \mathrm{I} \mathrm{I} \mathrm{I}, \end{array}\)

where \(w = (\mathbb{1} - \Delta)^{\gamma / 2 + \rho / 2 - d / 4}\). We bound \(\mathrm{I}\) by using Young's inequality and the Poisson summation formula \((\mathfrak{C}_s^{\varepsilon} - \mathfrak{C}_s^{L, \varepsilon}) (x ; z) = - \sum_{m \in \mathbb{Z}^d \setminus \{ 0 \}} \mathfrak{C}_s^{\varepsilon} (x ; z + m L)\), so that \(\mathrm{I} \lesssim 2^{- 2 \rho s} 2^{- \bar{c} (2^s L)^{\delta} / 2} \| w \Delta_j (\cdot - x) \|^2_{\mathfrak{h}}\). We bound \(\mathrm{I} \mathrm{I}\) by using the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality and by noting that for \(x \in (B_L)^c\)

\(\displaystyle \| \mathfrak{C}_s^{\varepsilon} (x ; \cdot) w^{- 1} \boldsymbol{1}_{B_{L / 2}} \|^2_{\mathfrak{h}_L} \lesssim 2^{(d - 2 \rho) s} \mathrm{e}^{- \bar{c} (2^s | x |)^{\delta} / 2},\)

which can be integrated in \(\mathrm{d} x\) and thus \(\mathrm{I} \mathrm{I} \lesssim 2^{- 2 \rho s} 2^{- \bar{c} (2^s L)^{\delta} / 2} \| w \Delta_j (\cdot - x) \|^2_{\mathfrak{h}}\). Finally, we estimate the last term by using separately the decay of \(\mathfrak{C}_s^{\varepsilon}\) and \(\mathfrak{C}_s^{L, \varepsilon}\) so that \(\| (\mathfrak{C}_s^{\varepsilon} - \mathfrak{C}_s^{L, \varepsilon}) w^{- 1} \|^2_{L^1 (\mathbb{R}^d ; \mathbb{C}^4)} \lesssim 2^{- 2 \rho s}\) and \(\mathrm{I} \mathrm{I} \mathrm{I} \lesssim 2^{- 2 \rho s} \| \boldsymbol{1}_{(B_{L / 2})^c} w \Delta_j (\cdot - x) \|^2_{\mathfrak{h}}\). The terms \(\mathrm{I}\) and \(\mathrm{I} \mathrm{I}\) decay already in \(L\). Concerning the term \(\mathrm{I} \mathrm{I} \mathrm{I}\), denote by \(K_j\) the kernel of \(w \Delta_j\) and note the faster than any power decay \(| K_j (x ; y) | \lesssim \frac{2^{(\gamma + \rho + d) j / 2} C_N}{1 + 2^j | x - y |^N}\), \(N \in \mathbb{N}\). Therefore

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} \sup_{x \in \mathbb{R}^d} \varrho_{\eta} (x) \left( \int_0^t \mathrm{I} \mathrm{I} \mathrm{I} \mathrm{d} s \right)^{1 / 2} & \lesssim & \rho^{- 1 / 2} \sup_{x \in \mathbb{R}^d} \varrho_{\eta} (x) \| \boldsymbol{1}_{(B_{L / 2})^c} w \Delta_j (\cdot - x) \|_{\mathfrak{h}}\\ & \lesssim & \rho^{- 1 / 2} L^{- \eta} 2^{(\gamma + \rho) j} . \end{array}\)

This concludes the proof.\(\Box\)

4Solution in the subcritical regime

In Section 4.1 we introduce Banach spaces of Grassmann polynomials as suitable multilinear operators in the fields, possibly with derivatives. In Section 4.2 we truncate the Polchinski flow equation and study global polynomial solutions in such Banach spaces. Note that the closer to the critical regime (that is, the closer \(\gamma\) is to \(d / 4\)), the less we can truncate the flow equation. Furthermore, the less we truncate the equation, the more regular is the remainder produced by the truncation. In Section 4.3 we prove global existence of the pair \((\Psi, R)\) by a fixed-point argument, compare with Lemma 3.22, prove Theorem 1.1 and, as a byproduct, prove the existence of the weak limit \(\omega^V\) stated in Theorem 1.3. Finally, in Section 4.4 we show the exponential decay of clustered correlations and the short-distance divergence of the two-point function and thus prove Theorem 1.3.

4.1Spaces of Grassmann monomials

We solve the truncated flow equation in suitable spaces of multilinear operators on fields and their derivatives. For technical reasons, it will suffice to consider fields with either no or two derivatives. Accordingly, to keep track of the derivatives, we henceforth consider only tuples of the form \(m = (m_i)_{i = 1}^k \in \{ 0, 2 \}^k\) for some \(k \in \mathbb{N}\). If \(m = (m_i)_{i = 1}^k\) for some \(k \in \mathbb{N}\), we denote its length by \(| m | := k\) and use \([m] := \sum_{i = 1}^k m_i\) to count the total number of derivatives. We will also consider the empty tuple, denoted by \(\emptyset\). We naturally set \(| \emptyset | = [\emptyset] = 0\). Letting \(m = (m_i)_{i = 1}^k\), \(m = (m_i')_{i = 1}^{k'}\) and \(m'' = (m_i'')_{i = 1}^{k''}\) be possibly empty tuples:

  • \(m' \subseteq m\), if \(m'\) can be formed by possibly removing elements from \(m\); note that \(m \subseteq m\) and \(\emptyset \subseteq m\) for any possibly empty tuple \(m\).

  • \(m \circ m'\) denotes the concatenated tuple obtained by juxtaposition, that is, \(m \circ m' = ((m \circ m')_i)_{i = 1}^{k + k'}\) with \((m \circ m')_i = m_i\) for \(i = 1, \ldots, k\) and \((m \circ m')_i = m'_i\) for \(i = k + 1, \ldots, k + k'\).

  • \(S_m\) denotes the set of permutations \(\pi\) of \((1, \ldots, k)\) such that \(m_{\pi (i)} = m_i ._{}\)

  • \(S^m_{m', m''}\) denotes the set of permutations of \((1, \ldots, k)\) into (\(I', I''\)) such that \(I' = (i'_1 \ldots i_{k'}')\) and \(I'' = (i''_1 \ldots i''_{k''})\) are ordered (that is, \(i'_1 < \ldots < i_{k'}'\) and \(i''_1 < \ldots < i''_{k''}\)) and such that \(m' = (m_{i'_j})_{j = 1}^{k'}\) and \(m'' = (m_{i''_j})_{j = 1}^{k''}\). In particular, if either \(m'\) or \(m''\) are empty, \(S^m_{m', m''}\) consists of the trivial permutation only.

Remark 4.1. Note the following binomial expansion, for \(\psi, \varphi\) odd variables,

\(\displaystyle (\psi + \varphi)^{\otimes (m)} = \sum_{m', m'' \subseteq m} \sum_{\pi \in S_{m', m''}^m} s (\pi) P_{\pi} (\psi^{\otimes (m')} \otimes \varphi^{\otimes (m'')}),\)

where, \(s\) is a suitable sign depending on \(\pi\), \(P_{\pi}\) lifts the action of \(\pi\) to \((P_{\pi} \psi) ((x_1, \mu_1) ; \ldots ; (x_k, \mu_k)) = \psi ((x_{\pi (1)}, \mu_{\pi (1)}) ; \ldots ; (x_{\pi (k)}, \mu_{\pi (k)}))\). Note of course that the right-hand side is vanishing if \(| S^m_{m', m''} | = 0\).

Let \(\mathcal{B} (V ; W)\) denote the Banach space of bounded linear operators from V to \(W\). We are interested in the following spaces of linear operators

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{L}^{(k, m)}_t := \mathcal{B} \left( \mathcal{C}^m_t (\mathbb{R}^{dk} ; \mathcal{M}^{4^k}) ; L^{\infty} (\mathbb{R}^d ; \mathcal{M}^4)^{} \right),\)

where \(k \in \mathbb{N}\) and \(m\) is a tuple of length \(k\). With any operator \({K \in \mathcal{L}^{(k, m)}_t}_{}\), we can associate an integral kernel, which is generally a distribution. For example, for any tuple \(m = (m_i)_{i = 1}^k\) we can write

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{l} (K_m (\psi^{(m)})) (y ; \mu)\\ \quad = \sum_{\text{\scriptsize{$\begin{array}{c} \mu_1, \ldots, \mu_k\\ \mu_i \in \{ \uparrow, \downarrow \} \times \{ \pm \} \end{array}$}}} \sum_{\text{\scriptsize{$\begin{array}{c} \nu_1, \ldots, \nu_k :\\ \nu_i \in \{ 1, \ldots, d \}^{m_i} \end{array}$}}} \int_{\mathbb{R}^{dk}} K_m ((y, \mu) ; (x_1, \mu_1, \nu_1) ; \cdots ; (x_k, \mu_k, \nu_k)) \times\\ \hspace{17em} \times \partial^{m_1}_{\nu_1} \otimes \cdots \partial^{m_k}_{\nu_k} \otimes \psi_{x_1, \mu_1 ; \ldots ; x_k, \mu_k} \mathrm{d} x_1 \cdots \mathrm{d} x_k, \end{array}\)

In our setting, such kernels are a.e. antisymmetric and invariant under translations and reflections across coordinate planes, that is, for any permutation \(\pi \in S_m\) and any \(z \in \mathbb{R}^d\)

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{l} K_m ((y, \mu) ; (x_1, \mu_1, \nu_1) ; \cdots ; (x_k, \mu_k, \nu_k))\\ \, = K_m ((y - z, \mu) ; (x_1 - z, \mu_1, \nu_1) ; \ldots ; (x_k - z, \mu_k, \nu_k))\\ \, = K_m ((- y, \mu) ; (- x_1, \mu_1, \nu_1) ; \cdots ; (- x_k, \mu_k {,} \nu_k))\\ \, =\operatorname{sign} (\pi) K_m ((y, \mu) ; (x_{\pi (1)}, \mu_{\pi (1)}, \nu_{\pi (1)}) ; \cdots ; (x_{\pi (k)}, \mu_{\pi (k)}, \nu_{\pi (k)})) . \end{array}\)

The multilinear operators we consider exhibit scale-dependent spatial decay in the separation of points. To keep track of this decay, we introduce the following scale-dependent weight, which is a generalisation of (3.37),

\(\displaystyle w^{(k)}_{t ; c} (x_1 ; \ldots ; x_k) := \mathrm{e}^{c (2^t \operatorname{St} (x_1 ; \ldots ; x_k))^{\delta}},\)

for \(c \geqslant 0\), \(\delta\) as in Definition 3.16, where \(\operatorname{St} (x_1 ; \ldots ; x_k)\) denotes the Steiner diameter of the set \(\{ x_1, \ldots, x_k \}\), see, e.g., [GMR21]. If \(K \in \mathcal{L}^{(k, m)}_t\), we let \(K \cdot w^{(k + 1)}_{t ; c}\) be the operator whose kernel is a.e. the point-wise product of the kernel of \(K\) with \(w^{(k + 1)}_{t : c} (x_1 ; \ldots ; x_{k + 1})\). Equivalently,

\(\displaystyle ((K \cdot w_{t ; c}^{(k + 1)}) (\psi^{(m)})) (y ; \mu) := (K (w_{t ; c}^{(k + 1)} (y ; \cdot) \psi^{(m)})) (y ; \mu) .\)

We introduce

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{L}^{(k, m)}_t (s ; c) := \left\{ K \in \mathcal{L}^{(k, m)}_t | \, \| K \cdot w^{(k + 1)}_{s ; c} \|_{\mathcal{L}^{(k, m)}_t} < \infty \right\},\)

and for \(K \in \mathcal{L}^{(k, m)}_t (s ; c)\) let \(\| K \|_{\mathcal{L}^{(k, m)}_t (s ; c)} := \| K \cdot w^{(k + 1)}_{s ; c} \|_{\mathcal{L}^{(k, m)}_t}\). We finally define the spaces of monomials with spatial decay in the separation of points.

Definition 4.2. Let \(k \in \mathbb{N}\) and \(m = (m_i)_i \in \{ 0, 2 \}^k\). The weighted space of monomials of degree \(k \in \mathbb{N}\) at scale \(t \geqslant 0\) is defined as the following subspace of the direct sum of \((\mathcal{L}^{(k, m)}_t)_m\):

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{L}^{(k)}_t (s ; c) := \left\{ (K_m)_m \in \bigoplus_{m \in \{ 0, 2 \}^k} \mathcal{L}^{(k, m)}_t (s ; c) | \, \operatorname{antisym}., \operatorname{trans}.\&\operatorname{refl}.\operatorname{invariant} \right\} .\)

The space \(\mathcal{L}^{(k)}_t (s ; c)\) is equipped with the norm

\(\displaystyle \| K \|_{\mathcal{L}^{(k)}_t (s ; c)} = \sup_{m \in \{ 0, 2 \}^k} 2^{[m]_{} (s - t)} \| K_m \|_{\mathcal{L}^{(k, m)}_t (s ; c)} . \) (4.1)

Finally, we let \(\mathcal{L}^{(k)}_t := \mathcal{L}^{(k)}_t (0 ; 0)\) denote the space of monomials of degree \(k\), at scale \(t\).

Remark 4.3. Note that for any \(s \geqslant s'\) and \(K \in \mathcal{L}^{(k)}_t (s ; c)\)

\(\displaystyle \| K \|_{\mathcal{L}^{(k)}_t (s' ; c)} \leqslant \| K \|_{\mathcal{L}^{(k)}_t (s ; c)}\)

and thus \(\mathcal{L}^{(k)}_t (s ; c) \subset \mathcal{L}^{(k)}_t (s' ; c)\), which follows because \(w^{(k)}_{s' ; c} \leqslant w^{(k)}_{s ; c}\).

Remark 4.4. Loosely speaking, in the norm \(\| \cdot \|_{\mathcal{L}^{(k)}_t (s ; c)}\), \(t\) is the scale of the fields, whereas \(s\) is the scale of the monomial itself. For controlling the flow equation, it is crucial to take \(t \leqslant s\) (except for the local monomial of degree one, see the next section). Since the field scale is not larger than the monomial one, the renormalisation operator gives a gain, as can be seen in eq. (4.5) below and motivates the choice of the scale-dependent weight in \(\| \cdot \|_{\mathcal{L}^{(k)}_t (s ; c)}\).

As long as cut-offs are present, functions in the Grassmann fields are polynomials and can be therefore written as \(F_t (\psi_t) := \sum_k F_t^{(k)} (\psi_t)\), where the sum contains only finitely many terms, \(\psi_t\) being a Grassmann field and \(F^{(k)} {\in \mathcal{L}^{(k)}_t}_{}\), so that

\(\displaystyle F_t^{(k)} (\psi_t) = \sum_{m \in \{ 0, 2 \}^k} F_t^{(k, m)} (\psi_t^{\otimes (m)}) . \) (4.2)

This allows us to lift the flow equation to the space of sequences \((F^{(k)})_{k \geqslant 1}\), see Section 4.2 for details. Note, however, that the expansion (4.2) is not unique, see also Remark 4.6 below, and we will exploit this flexibility to write the lifted flow equation in such a way that the correct boundary data can be obtained, that is, the correct local “renormalised counterterm” in the interacting measure.

We now introduce two operators on \(\mathcal{L}_t^{(1)}\) that allow us to rearrange the expansion in (4.2), namely the so-called localisation and renormalisation operators. These operators are well-known in the study of the renormalisation group flow, see, e.g., [Mas08, BBS19] and are crucial for our purposes because, as explained before, allow us to obtain non-trivial local boundary terms in the flow equation.

Definition 4.5. (Localisation & Renormalisation). The localisation and renormalisation operators are respectively the projections \(\operatorname{Loc}\) and \(\operatorname{Ren}\) onto \(\mathcal{L}^{(1)}_t\)defined as follows: if \(K = (K_0, K_2)\), we let

\(\displaystyle \operatorname{Loc}K := (\operatorname{Loc}_0 K_0, 0), \hspace{4em} \operatorname{Ren}K := (0, K_2 + (\mathbb{1} -\operatorname{Loc}_0) K_0),\)

where

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{l} (\operatorname{Loc}_0 K_0) ((y ; \mu) ; (x ; \mu')) := \delta (y - x) \int_{\mathbb{R}^d} K ((y, \mu) ; (z ; \mu')) \mathrm{d} z \end{array}\)

with \(\delta (y - x)\) denoting the Dirac delta distribution.

Remark 4.6. Consider \(K (\psi) := \sum_{m \in \{ 0, 2 \}^k} K_m (\psi^{(m)})\). Note that even though \(\operatorname{Loc}+\operatorname{Ren} \neq \mathbb{1}\) on \(\mathcal{L}^{(1)}_t\), we still have the identity \(K (\psi) =\operatorname{Loc}K (\psi) +\operatorname{Ren}K (\psi)\). In other words, we have the equivalent relation \(K \sim (\operatorname{Loc}+\operatorname{Ren}) K = (\operatorname{Loc}_0 K_0, K_2 + (\mathbb{1} -\operatorname{Loc}_0) K_0)\), which we will use to properly rewrite the lifted flow equation on \(\mathcal{L}_t^{(k)}\).

In view of the application to the flow equation, we provide the following lemma.

Lemma 4.7. For any \(r, s, t \geqslant 0\) and any \(K \in \mathcal{L}^{(1)}_t\) we have

\(\displaystyle \| \operatorname{Loc}K \|_{\mathcal{L}^{(1)}_r (s ; c)} = \| \operatorname{Loc}K \|_{\mathcal{L}^{(1)}_t (s ; c)} \leqslant \| K \|_{\mathcal{L}^{(1)}_t} . \) (4.3)

If \(K \in \mathcal{L}^{(1)}_t (s ; c)\), then for any \(c' < c\)

\(\displaystyle \| \operatorname{Ren}K \|_{\mathcal{L}^{(1)}_t (s ; c')} \lesssim_{c - c'} \| K \|_{\mathcal{L}^{(1)}_t (s ; c)} . \) (4.4)

Proof. The identity and the inequality in (4.3) follow by noting that \((\operatorname{Loc}K)_2 = 0\) and that \(w^{(2)}_{s ; \delta ; c} (y ; y) = 1\). To prove (4.4), one should note that, for \(\psi\) smooth enough,

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{l} ((\mathbb{1} -\operatorname{Loc}_0) K_0 (\psi)) (y ; \mu)_{}\\ \quad = \sum_{\mu'} \int_{\mathbb{R}^d} K_0 ((y, \mu) ; (x ; \mu')) \left[ \psi^{} (x ; \mu') - \psi (y ; \mu') - (x - y) \cdot \partial \psi (y ; \mu') \right] \mathrm{d} x\\ \quad = \sum_{\mu'} \int_{\mathbb{R}^d} K_0 ((y, \mu) ; (x ; \mu')) \int_0^1 (1 - t) (x - y)^2 \cdot \partial^2 \psi (y + t (x - y) ; \mu') \mathrm{d} t \mathrm{d} x, \end{array}\)

where in the second line we used that \(K_0\) is translation and reflection invariant, so that \(\int_{\mathbb{R}^d} K_0 ((y, \mu) ; (x ; \mu')) (x - y) \mathrm{d} y = 0\), and where in the last line the tensors \((x - y)^2\) and \(\partial^2\) are contracted in the internal indices. We have

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{l} \| (\mathbb{1} -\operatorname{Loc}_0) K_0 \|_{\mathcal{L}^{(1, (2))}_t (s ; c')}\\ \quad \leqslant 2^{2 t} \sup_{y, \mu} \sum_{\mu'} \int_{\mathbb{R}^d} \int_0^1 (1 - t) | K_0 ((y, \mu) ; (x ; \mu')) (x - y)^2 | w^{(2)}_{s ; c'} (y ; y + t (x - y)) \mathrm{d} t \mathrm{d} x\\ \quad \leqslant 2^{2 t} \sup_{y, \mu} \sum_{\mu'} \int_{\mathbb{R}^d} \int_0^1 (1 - t) | K_0 ((y, \mu) ; (x ; \mu')) (x - y)^2 | w^{(2)}_{s ; c'} (y ; x) \mathrm{d} t \mathrm{d} x\\ \quad \lesssim 2^{2 (t - s)} \sup_{y, \mu} \sum_{\mu'} \int_{\mathbb{R}^d} | K_0 ((y, \mu) ; (x ; \mu')) | w^{(2)}_{s ; c} (y ; x) \mathrm{d} x\\ \quad = 2^{2 (t - s)} \| K_0 \|_{\mathcal{L}^{(1, (0))}_t (s ; c)}, \end{array} \) (4.5)

where \(\lesssim\) is up to constants depending on \(c - c'\), implying the claim.\(\Box\)

We conclude this section by introducing the Laplacian and the contraction operators. Loosely speaking, these operators implement the action of \(\frac{1}{2} \mathrm{D}_{\dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon}}^2\) and of \(\langle \dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon} \cdot, \mathrm{D} \cdot \rangle\) on the space of monomials described above. In the definition below, we think of \(\dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon}\) as an element of \(C^{\infty} (\mathbb{R}^d \times \mathbb{R}^d ; \mathbb{C}^{4 \times 4})\) and thus write \(\dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon (m)}\) for some \(m \in \{ 0, 2 \}^2\).

Definition 4.8. The Laplacian operator is the linear operator \(\mathbb{D}^2_{\dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon}} : \mathcal{L}^{(k + 2)}_t \rightarrow \mathcal{L}^{(k)}_t\) for any \(k \geqslant 1\) that acts as follows: if \(K \in \mathcal{L}^{(k + 2, m)}_t\) and \(m' \subset m\), with \(| m' | = k\)

\(\displaystyle (\mathbb{D}^2_{\dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon}} K)_{m'} (\psi^{(m')}) := \sum_{m'' \subseteq m} \sum_{\pi \in S^m_{m', m''}} s (\pi) K_m (P_{\pi} \psi^{(m')} \otimes \dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon (m'')}) .\)

where, \(s (\pi) \in \{ \pm 1 \}\) being a sign due to the anticommutativity of the fields. The contraction operator is the bilinear operator \(\mathbb{C}_{\dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon}} : \mathcal{L}^{(k + 1)}_t \times \mathcal{L}^{(k')}_t \rightarrow \mathcal{L}^{(k + k')}_t\), for any \(k, k' \geqslant 1\) that acts as follows: if \(K \in \mathcal{L}^{(k + 1, m)}_t\), \(K' \in \mathcal{L}^{(k', m')}_t\) and \(m'' \subset m\), with \(| m'' | = k\)

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{l} (\mathbb{C}_{\dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon}} (K, K'))_{m'', \, m'} (\psi^{(m'' \circ m')})\\ \quad := \sum_{\bar{m} \subseteq m} \sum_{\pi \in S^m_{m'', \bar{m}}} s' (\pi) \sum_{\mu'} \int K_m (P_{\pi} \psi^{(m'')} \otimes \dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon (\bar{m})} ((y' ; \mu'), \cdot)) \, \cdot \, K'_{m'} (\psi^{(m')}) ((y' ; \mu')) \mathrm{d} y' \end{array}\)

where \(s' (\pi) \in \{ \pm 1 \}\) is another suitable sign related to the permutation \(\pi\).

In view of the application to the flow equation the following lemma is crucial.

Lemma 4.9. If \(K \in \mathcal{L}^{(k + 2)}_t (s ; c)\), then \(\mathbb{D}^2_{\dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon}} (K) \in \mathcal{L}^{(k)}_t (s ; c)\) and

\(\displaystyle \| \mathbb{D}^2_{\dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon}} (K) \|_{\mathcal{L}^{(k)}_t (s ; c)} \lesssim k^2 {\| K \|_{\mathcal{L}^{(k + 2)}_t (s ; c)}}_{} \| \dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon} \|_{L^{\infty}} . \) (4.6)

If \(K \in \mathcal{L}^{(k + 1)}_t (s ; c)\) and \(K' \in \mathcal{L}^{(k')}_t (s ; c)\), then \(\mathbb{C}_{\dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon}} (K, K') \in \mathcal{L}^{(k + k')}_t (s ; c)\)

\(\displaystyle \| \mathbb{C}_{\dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon}} (K, K') \|_{\mathcal{L}^{(k + k')}_t (s ; c)} \lesssim k {\| K \|_{\mathcal{L}^{(k + 1)}_t (s ; c)}}_{} \| K' \|_{\mathcal{L}^{(k')}_t (s ; c)} _{} \| \dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon} \|_{L^1_{s ; c}} . \) (4.7)

Proof. The bound on the Laplacian operator follows by noting that there are at most \((k + 1) (k + 2)\) elements in the sum and by noting that

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} \left\| K_m \left( P_{\pi} \left( \cdot \; \dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon (m'')} \right) \right) \right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{(k, m')}_t (s ; c)} & = & \left\| \left| K_m \left( P_{\pi} \left( \cdot \; \dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon (m'')} \right) \right) \right| \cdot w^{(k + 1)}_{s ; c} \right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{(k, m')}_t}\\ & \leqslant & 2^{- [m''] t} \left\| {\dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon (m'')}}^{} \right\|_{L^{\infty}} \| | K_m | \cdot w^{(k + 3)}_{s ; c} \|_{\mathcal{L}^{(k + 2, m)}_t}\\ & \lesssim & 2^{[m''] (s - t)} \| \dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon} \|_{L^{\infty}} \| K_m \|_{\mathcal{L}^{(k + 2, m)}_t (s ; c)}, \end{array}\)

where in the last line we used that \(\left\| {\dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon (m'')}}^{} \right\|_{L^{\infty}} \lesssim 2^{[m''] s} \| \dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon} \|_{L^{\infty}}\), see Corollary 3.24, and that the Steiner diameter is a non-decreasing function in the cardinality of the set. To prove the bound on the contraction operator, we observe that there are at most \(k + 1\) terms in the sum and that

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{l} \left\| \int K_m \left( P_{\pi} \left( \cdot \, \dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon (\bar{m})} ((y, \mu), \cdot) \right) \right) K'_{m'} ((y, \mu)) \mathrm{d} y \right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{(k + k', m'' \circ m')}_t (s ; c)}\\ \quad = {\left\| \left| \int K_m \left( P_{\pi} \left( \cdot \, \dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon (\bar{m})} ((y, \mu), \cdot) \right) \right) K'_{m'} ((y, \mu)) \mathrm{d} y \right| \cdot w^{(k + k' + 1)}_{s ; c} \right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{(k + k', m'' \circ m')}_t}}_{}\\ \quad \leqslant \left\| \int \left| K_m \left( P_{\pi} \left( \cdot \, \dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon (\bar{m})} ((y, \mu), \cdot) \right) \right) \right| \mathrm{d} y \cdot w^{(k + 2)}_{s ; c} (y ; \cdot) \right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{(k, m'')}_t} \| K'_{m'} \cdot w^{(k' + 1)}_{s ; c} \|_{\mathcal{L}^{(k', m')}_t}\\ \quad \leqslant 2^{- [\bar{m}] t} \left\| {\dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon (\bar{m})}}^{} \right\|_{L^1_{s ; c}} \| K_m \cdot w^{(k + 2)}_{s ; c} \|_{\mathcal{L}^{(k + 1, m'')}_t} \| K'_{m'} \cdot w^{(k' + 1)}_{s ; c} \|^{}_{\mathcal{L}^{(k', m')}_t}\\ \quad \lesssim 2^{[\bar{m}] (s - t)} \left\| {\dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon}}^{} \right\|_{L^1_{s ; c}} \| K_m \cdot w^{(k + 2)}_{s ; c} \|_{\mathcal{L}^{(k + 1, m)}_t} \| K'_{m'} \cdot w^{(k' + 1)}_{s ; c} \|^{}_{\mathcal{L}^{(k', m')}_t}, \end{array}\)

where in the third line we used that \(\operatorname{St} (\{ x, x_1, \ldots, x_k \} \cup \{ x'_1, \ldots, x_{k'}' \}) \leqslant \operatorname{St} (\{ x, x_1, \ldots, x_k, y \}) +\operatorname{St} (\{ y, x'_1, \ldots, x_{k'}' \})\) and in the fourth line that \(\operatorname{St} (\{ x, x_1, \ldots, x_k, y \}) \leqslant \operatorname{St} (\{ x, x_1, \ldots, x_k \}) + | x_k - y |\).\(\Box\)

Remark 4.10. Note that the scale dependent weight in the norm \(\| \cdot \|_{\mathcal{L}^{(k + k')}_t (s ; c)}\), see (4.1) are crucial for these bounds to hold. In fact, when the derivatives act on \(\dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon}\) one loses \(2^s\) for each derivative, but this factor is then cancelled by the gain in the kernel, i.e., due to the weight in (4.1). The following corollary is an immediate consequence of Lemma 4.9 and Lemma 4.7.

Corollary 4.11. If \(K \in \mathcal{L}^{(3)}_t (s ; c)\), then for any \(c' < c\), \(\operatorname{Ren}\mathbb{D}^2_{\dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon}} (K) \in \mathcal{L}_t^{(1)} (s ; c')\) and

\(\displaystyle \| (\operatorname{Ren}\mathbb{D}^2_{\dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon}} (K))_2 \|_{\mathcal{L}^{(1, (2))}_t (s ; c')} \lesssim 2^{- 2 (s - t)} {\| K \|_{\mathcal{L}^{(3)}_t (s ; c)}}_{} \| \dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon} \|_{L^{\infty}_{s ; c}} .\)

If \(K, K' \in \mathcal{L}^{(1)}_t (s ; c)\), then for any \(c' < c\), \(\operatorname{Ren}\mathbb{C}_{\dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon}} (K, K') \in \mathcal{L}_t^{(1)} (s ; c')\) and

\(\displaystyle \| (\operatorname{Ren}\mathbb{C}^2_{\dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon}} (K, K'))_2 \|_{\mathcal{L}^{(1, (2))}_t (s ; c')} \lesssim_{c - c'} 2^{- 2 (s - t)} {\| K \|_{\mathcal{L}^{(1)}_t (s ; c)}}_{} {\| K' \|_{\mathcal{L}^{(1)}_t (s ; c)}}_{} \| \dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon} \|_{L^1_{s ; c}} .\)

4.2The truncated flow equation

For technical convenience, we study the flow equation by introducing a further grading with respect to the derivative in the flow parameter. Thus, we decompose the function \((F_s^{\varepsilon})_s\) as follows

\(\displaystyle F_s^{\varepsilon} (\psi_t) = \sum_{\ell \geqslant 0} F_s^{\varepsilon [\ell]} (\psi_t) = \sum_{\ell \geqslant 0, k \geqslant 1} F_s^{\varepsilon [\ell] (k)} (\psi_t), \) (4.8)

where the label \(\ell\) is the grading with respect to the operator \(\partial_s\), whereas \(k\) is the grading with respect to the functional derivative \(\mathrm{D}\), so that \(F_s^{\varepsilon [\ell] (k)} \in \mathcal{L}^{(k)}_s\) are monomials of degree \(k\). This grading decomposition is natural because the flow equation involves both differential operators. We introduce the following truncated flow equation

\(\displaystyle \partial_s F^{\varepsilon}_s (\psi_s) + \frac{1}{2} \mathrm{D}_{\dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon}}^2 F^{\varepsilon}_s (\psi_s) + \Pi_{\leqslant n} \mathrm{D} F^{\varepsilon}_s (\psi_s) \cdot \dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon} F^{\varepsilon}_s (\psi_s) = 0, \) (4.9)

where \(\Pi_{\leqslant n}\) is the projection on terms with \(\ell \leqslant n\). For convenience, we also introduce the notation

\(\displaystyle H_{> n ; t}^{\varepsilon} (\psi_t) := \Pi_{> n} \mathrm{D} F^{\varepsilon}_t (\psi_t) \cdot \dot{G}_t^{\varepsilon} F^{\varepsilon}_t (\psi_t) . \) (4.10)

The truncation is convenient because it allows us to look for polynomial solutions. Note that we could truncate with respect to both gradings, with no major difference in the analysis. Expanding as in (4.8), the truncated flow equation reads, for \(\ell = 0, \ldots, n\):

\(\displaystyle \partial_s F_s^{\varepsilon [\ell + 1] (k)} +\mathbb{D}^2_{\dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon}} (F_s^{\varepsilon [\ell] (k + 2)}) + \sum_{\ell' = 0}^{\ell} \sum_{k' = 0}^{k - 1} \mathbb{C}_{\dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon}} (F_s^{\varepsilon [\ell'] (k - k')}, F_s^{\varepsilon [\ell - \ell'] (k' + 1)}) = 0, \) (4.11)

where the operators \(\mathbb{D}^2_{\dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon}}\) and \(\mathbb{C}_{\dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon}}\) are as in Definition 4.8. Note that eq. (4.11) is triangular in \(\ell\) and this will bring technical simplification to the analysis.

We let

\(\displaystyle c_{\ell} := (1 - \ell n^{- 1}) \bar{c} / 2, \) (4.12)

\(\bar{c}\) being the constant in Lemma 3.23. We make the following Ansatz for the norm of the monomials \(F_s^{\varepsilon [\ell] (k)}\), for suitable constants \(C > 0\), \(\alpha, \beta, \kappa \geqslant 0\):

\(\displaystyle \| F_s^{\varepsilon [\ell] (k)} \|_{\mathcal{L}^{(k)}_t (s ; c_{\ell})} \lesssim C^k 2^{(\alpha - \beta k - \kappa \ell) s}, \qquad 0 \leqslant t \leqslant s.\) (4.13)

If we interpret the exponent multiplying \(s\) as the regularity, we have that \(\beta\) quantifies the gain in regularity due to the grading in the fields \(k\) whereas \(\kappa\) the gain due to the perturbative order in \(\ell\). Because the problem is super-renormalisable, we will have \(\beta > \gamma\), \(- \gamma\) being the regularity of the fields and furthermore \(\kappa > 0\). Now note that because of the “initial condition” \(F^{[0]}_s (\psi^{\varepsilon}_s) = \lambda (\psi^{\varepsilon}_s)^3\), the Ansatz (4.13) can hold true only if

\(\displaystyle \alpha - 3 \beta \geqslant 0. \) (4.14)

Furthermore, as shall see shortly, these estimates can be propagated only if the following conditions are satisfied:

\(\displaystyle 5 \beta - \alpha - 2 \gamma - \kappa > 0, \qquad d + 4 \beta - 2 \alpha - 2 \gamma - \kappa > 0, \) (4.15)

and

\(\displaystyle 3 \beta + 2 - \alpha - 2 \gamma - \kappa > 0, \qquad d + 2 \beta + 2 - 2 \alpha - 2 \gamma - \kappa > 0 \) (4.16)

The constraints in (4.15) makes irrelevant the terms with \(k \geqslant 3\), in the sense of integrability at \(s = \infty\) of the Laplacian and contraction operators respectively. On the other hand, the constraints in (4.16) make irrelevant the renormalised term with \(k = 1\).

Remark 4.12. Note that the constraints (4.14), (4.15) and (4.16) can be satisfied only provided that

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{l} \gamma < \min \{ d / 4, 1 \}, \qquad \beta \in (\gamma, \min \{ d / 2 - \gamma, d / 4 + 1 / 2 - \gamma / 2 \}),\\ \alpha \in [3 \beta, d / 2 + \min \{ 2 \beta, \beta + 1 \} - \gamma) . \end{array} \) (4.17)

The condition \(\gamma < d / 4\) is what makes the model super-renormalisable. As hinted in the introduction, the constraint \(\gamma < 1\) comes from the requirement that the counter-term is a mass term and does not have derivatives of the fields. Note that the super-renormalisability of the model allows for room in the choice of the parameters \(\beta, \alpha\) and \(\kappa\).

Given the Ansatz (4.13), the following norm topology is natural: for any graded sequence \(F = (F_s^{[\ell] (k)})_{s \geqslant 0}^{\ell \geqslant 0, k \geqslant 0}\), we introduce the norm

\(\displaystyle \| F \|_{C, \alpha, \beta, \kappa} := \sup_{\ell \geqslant 0, k \geqslant 0} \sup_{s, t : \, 0 \leqslant t \leqslant s} C^{- k} 2^{- (\alpha - \beta k - \kappa \ell) s} \| F_s^{[\ell] (k)} \|_{\mathcal{L}^{(k)}_t (s ; c_{\ell})}, \) (4.18)

where for simplicity we dropped the dependence on \((c_{\ell})_{\ell}\). The following theorem establishes the existence and uniqueness of a global solution of the truncated flow equations.

Theorem 4.13. (Global solution). Let \(\varepsilon \in 2^{-\mathbb{N}_{\infty}}\), \(n \in \mathbb{N}\), \(C > 0\), let \(\alpha, \beta, \gamma, \kappa \geqslant 0\) satisfy the constraints in (4.14), (4.15), (4.16) and let \(c_{\ell}\) be as in (4.12). The truncated flow equations (4.11) with boundary data

\(\displaystyle F^{\varepsilon [0] (k)}_0 = K^{(k)} \boldsymbol{1}_{k \in \{ 1, 3 \}}, \qquad \qquad \sup_{k \geqslant 0} \sup_{0 \leqslant t \leqslant s} C^{- k} 2^{- (\alpha - \beta k) s} \| K^{(k)} \|_{\mathcal{L}^{(k)}_t (s ; c_0)} < \infty,\)

and for \(\ell \geqslant 1\)

\(\displaystyle F^{\varepsilon [\ell] (k)}_{\infty} = 0 \qquad \forall k \neq 1, \hspace{3em} \operatorname{Ren}F^{\varepsilon [\ell] (1)}_{\infty} = 0, \hspace{3em} \operatorname{Loc}F^{\varepsilon [\ell] (1)}_0 = 0\)

have a unique global solution \(F^{\varepsilon} = \left( F_s^{\varepsilon [\ell] (k)} \boldsymbol{1}_{k \hspace{1.2em} \operatorname{odd}} \right)_{s \geqslant 0}^{\ell \geqslant 0, k \geqslant 0}\), such that \(F_s^{\varepsilon [\ell] (k)} \in \mathcal{L}^{(k)}_t (s ; c_{\ell})\).

Remark 4.14. Note that the monomials \(K^{(k)}\) have to be local in order to satisfy the said bound.

Proof. The proof is by induction in \(\ell\). We let \(F^{\varepsilon [\leqslant \ell]}\) denote the graded sequence \((F_t^{\varepsilon [\ell'] (k)} \boldsymbol{1}_{\ell' \leqslant \ell})_{t \geqslant 0}^{\ell' \geqslant 0, k \geqslant 0}\) and show that \(\| F^{\varepsilon [\leqslant \ell]} \|_{C, \alpha, \beta, \kappa}\) is finite.

The validity at \(\ell = 0\) is obvious because \(\partial_s F^{\varepsilon [0]}_s = 0\) and because of the assumptions. In particular, we have that \(\| F^{\varepsilon [\leqslant 0]} \|_{C, \alpha, \beta, \kappa} < \infty\).To prove the induction step, we integrate the truncated flow equation (4.11) for \(k \geqslant 3\) backwards from the final condition at \(s = \infty\) with \(F_{\infty}^{\varepsilon [\ell + 1] (k)} = 0\) for any \(\ell \geqslant 0, k \geqslant 3\). We have:

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} F_t^{\varepsilon [\ell + 1] (k)} & = & \int_t^{\infty} \mathbb{D}_{\dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon}} (F_s^{\varepsilon [\ell] (k + 2)}) \mathrm{d} s\\ & & \quad + \int_t^{\infty} \sum_{\ell' = 0}^{\ell} \sum_{k' = 0}^{k - 1} \mathbb{C}_{\dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon}} (F_s^{\varepsilon [\ell'] (k - k')}, F_s^{\varepsilon [\ell - \ell'] (k' + 1)}) \mathrm{d} s \end{array}\)
\(\displaystyle \)

For the term with \(k = 1\), we use the equivalence \(F_t^{\varepsilon [\ell + 1] (1)} \sim \operatorname{Loc}F_t^{\varepsilon [\ell + 1] (1)} +\operatorname{Ren}F_t^{\varepsilon [\ell + 1] (1)}\), that is, \(F_t^{\varepsilon [\ell + 1] (1)} \sim ((\operatorname{Loc}F_t^{\varepsilon [\ell + 1] (1)})_0, (\operatorname{Ren}F_t^{\varepsilon [\ell + 1] (1)})_2)\). We integrate the term with the renormalisation from \(s = \infty\), with \((\operatorname{Ren}F_{\infty}^{\varepsilon [\ell + 1] (1)})_2 = 0\) for any \(\ell \geqslant 0\)

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} (\operatorname{Ren}F_t^{\varepsilon [\ell + 1] (1)})_2 & = & \int_t^{\infty} (\operatorname{Ren}\mathbb{D}^2_{\dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon}} (F_s^{\varepsilon [\ell] (3)}))_2 \mathrm{d} s\\ & & \quad + \int_t^{\infty} \sum_{\ell' = 0}^{\ell} (\operatorname{Ren}\mathbb{C}_{\dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon}} (F_s^{\varepsilon [\ell'] (1)}, F_s^{\varepsilon [\ell - \ell'] (1)}))_2 \mathrm{d} s, \end{array}\)

and, finally, we integrate the term with the localisation from \(0\) to \(t\), with \((\operatorname{Loc}F_t^{\varepsilon [\ell + 1] (1)})_0 = 0\) for any \(\ell \geqslant 0\)

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} (\operatorname{Loc}F_t^{\varepsilon [\ell + 1] (1)})_0 & = & - \int_0^t (\operatorname{Loc}\mathbb{D}^2_{\dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon}} (F_s^{\varepsilon [\ell] (3)}))_0 \mathrm{d} s\\ & & \quad - \int_0^t \sum_{\ell' = 0}^{\ell} (\operatorname{Loc}\mathbb{C}_{\dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon}} (F_s^{\varepsilon [\ell'] (1)}, F_s^{\varepsilon [\ell - \ell'] (1)}))_0 \mathrm{d} s. \end{array}\)

Now, recalling the kernel estimates for the propagator \(\| \dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon} \|_{L^p_{s ; c}} \lesssim 2^{- (d / p - 2 \gamma) s}\), for \(c < \bar{c}\), see Corollary 3.24, Lemma 4.9 implies the following estimates:

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{l} \| \mathbb{D}^2_{\dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon}} (F_s^{\varepsilon [\ell] (k + 2)}) \|_{\mathcal{L}^{(k)}_t (s ; c_{\ell})}\\ \qquad \lesssim k^2 C^{k + 2} 2^{(\alpha - \beta k) s} 2^{2 (\gamma - \beta) s} 2^{- \kappa \ell s} \| F^{\varepsilon [\leqslant \ell]} \|_{C, \alpha, \beta, \kappa}, \end{array} \) (4.19)

and for \(\ell' = 0, \ldots, \ell\)

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{l} \| \mathbb{C}_{\dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon}} (F_s^{\varepsilon [\ell'] (k - k')}, F_s^{\varepsilon [\ell - \ell'] (k' + 1)}) \|_{\mathcal{L}^{(k + k')}_t (s ; c_{\ell})}\\ \hspace{7em} \lesssim k C^{k + 1} 2^{(\alpha - \beta k) s} 2^{- (d + \beta - \alpha - 2 \gamma) s} 2^{- \kappa \ell s} \| F^{\varepsilon [\leqslant \ell]} \|_{C, \alpha, \beta, \kappa}^2 . \end{array} \) (4.20)

By using these bounds, the constraints (4.17) and Remark 4.3, we obtain that for \(k \geqslant 3\) and \(r \leqslant t\) \(F_t^{\varepsilon [\ell + 1] (k)} \in \mathcal{L}^{(k)}_r (t ; c_{\ell + 1})\) and

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{l} {\| F_t^{\varepsilon [\ell + 1] (k)} \|_{\mathcal{L}^{(k)}_r (t ; c_{\ell + 1})}}_{}\\ \qquad \leqslant \int_t^{\infty} \| \mathbb{D}^2_{\dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon}} (F_s^{\varepsilon [\ell] (k + 2)}) \|_{\mathcal{L}^{(k)}_r (s ; c_{\ell})} \mathrm{d} s\\ \hspace{3em} + \int_t^{\infty} \sum_{\ell' = 0}^{\ell} \sum_{k' = 0}^{k - 2} {\| \mathbb{C}_{\dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon}} (F_s^{\varepsilon [\ell'] (k - k')}, F_s^{\varepsilon [\ell - \ell'] (k' + 1)}) \|_{\mathcal{L}^{(k)}_r (s ; c_{\ell})}}_{} \mathrm{d} s\\ \qquad \lesssim k^2 C^{k + 2} \|F^{\varepsilon [\leqslant \ell]} \|_{C, \alpha, \beta, \kappa} \int_t^{\infty} 2^{(\alpha - \beta k) s} 2^{2 (\gamma - \beta) s} 2^{- \kappa \ell s} \mathrm{d} s\\ \hspace{3em} + (\ell + 1) k C^{k + 1} \| F^{\varepsilon [\leqslant \ell]} \|_{C, \alpha, \beta, \kappa}^2 \int_t^{\infty} 2^{(\alpha - \beta k) s} 2^{- (d + \beta - \alpha - 2 \gamma) s} 2^{- \kappa \ell s} \mathrm{d} s\\ \qquad \lesssim C^{k + 1} 2^{(\alpha - \beta k) t} 2^{- \kappa (\ell + 1) t} (\| F^{\varepsilon [\leqslant \ell]} \|_{C, \alpha, \beta, \kappa} + \| F^{\varepsilon [\leqslant \ell]} \|_{C, \alpha, \beta, \kappa}^2), \end{array} \) (4.21)

where in the last line \(\lesssim\) is up to constants depending on \(\ell, \alpha, \beta, \gamma, \kappa\). In a similar way, Corollary 3.24, Corollary 4.11 with \(c' := c_{\ell + 1}\) and \(c := c_{\ell}\), and the constraints (4.17), we obtain that for \(r \leqslant t\) \((\operatorname{Ren}F_t^{\varepsilon [\ell + 1] (1)})_2 \in \mathcal{L}^{(1, (2))}_r (t ; c_{\ell + 1})\) and

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{l} \| (\operatorname{Ren}F_t^{\varepsilon [\ell + 1] (1)})_2 \|_{\mathcal{L}^{(1, (2))}_r (t ; c_{\ell + 1})}\\ \qquad \leqslant \int_t^{\infty} \| (\operatorname{Ren}\mathbb{D}^2_{\dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon}} (F_s^{\varepsilon [\ell] (3)}))_2 \|_{\mathcal{L}^{(1, (2))}_r (s ; c_{\ell + 1})} \mathrm{d} s\\ \hspace{3em} + \int_t^{\infty} \sum_{\ell' = 0}^{\ell} \| (\operatorname{Ren}\mathbb{C}_{\dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon}} (F_s^{\varepsilon [\ell'] (1)}, F_s^{\varepsilon [\ell - \ell'] (1)}))_2 \|_{\mathcal{L}^{(1, (2))}_r (s ; c_{\ell + 1})} \mathrm{d} s\\ \qquad \lesssim C^3 \| F^{\varepsilon [\leqslant \ell]} \|_{C, \alpha, \beta, \kappa} 2^{2 r} \int_t^{\infty} 2^{(\alpha - \beta - 2) s} 2^{2 (\gamma - \beta) s} 2^{- \kappa \ell s} \mathrm{d} s\\ \hspace{3em} + (\ell + 1) C^2 \| F^{\varepsilon [\leqslant \ell]} \|_{C, \alpha, \beta, \kappa}^2 2^{2 r} \int_t^{\infty} 2^{(\alpha - \beta - 2) s} 2^{- (d + \beta - \alpha - 2 \gamma) s} 2^{- \kappa \ell s} \mathrm{d} s\\ \qquad \lesssim C^2 2^{2 (r - t)} 2^{(\alpha - \beta) t} 2^{- \kappa (\ell + 1) t} (\| F^{\varepsilon [\leqslant \ell]} \|_{C, \alpha, \beta, \kappa} + \| F^{\varepsilon [\leqslant \ell]} \|_{C, \alpha, \beta, \kappa}^2) . \end{array} \) (4.22)

Then, we control the term with the localisation operator, for \(r \leqslant t\)

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{l} \| (\operatorname{Loc}F_t^{\varepsilon [\ell + 1] (1)})_0 \|_{\mathcal{L}^{(1, (0))}_r (t ; c_{\ell + 1})}\\ \quad \leqslant \int_0^t \| \operatorname{Loc}\mathbb{D}^2_{\dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon}} (F_s^{\varepsilon [\ell] (3)}) \|_{\mathcal{L}^{(1)}_0 (s ; c_{\ell})} \mathrm{d} s\\ \qquad + \sum_{\ell' = 0}^{\ell} \int_0^t \| \operatorname{Loc}\mathbb{C}_{\dot{G}_s^{\varepsilon}} (F_s^{\varepsilon [\ell'] (1)}, F_s^{\varepsilon [\ell - \ell'] (1)}) \|_{\mathcal{L}^{(1)}_0 (s ; c_{\ell})} \mathrm{d} s\\ \quad \lesssim C^3 \| F^{\varepsilon [\leqslant \ell]} \|_{C, \alpha, \beta, \kappa} \int_0^t 2^{(\alpha - \beta) s} 2^{2 (\gamma - \beta) s} 2^{- \kappa \ell s} \mathrm{d} s\\ \qquad + (\ell + 1) C^2 \| F^{\varepsilon [\leqslant \ell]} \|_{C, \alpha, \beta, \kappa}^2 \int_0^t 2^{(\alpha - \beta) s} 2^{- (d + \beta - \alpha - 2 \gamma) s} 2^{- \kappa \ell s} \mathrm{d} s\\ \quad \lesssim C^2 2^{(\alpha - \beta) t} 2^{- \kappa (\ell + 1) t} (\| F^{\varepsilon [\leqslant \ell]} \|_{C, \alpha, \beta, \kappa} + \| F^{\varepsilon [\leqslant \ell]} \|_{C, \alpha, \beta, \kappa}^2), \end{array} \) (4.23)

where we used that \(\| (K)_0 \|_{\mathcal{L}^{(1, (0))}_r (t ; c_{\ell + 1})} \leqslant \| K \|_{\mathcal{L}_r^{(1)} (t ; c_{\ell + 1})}\), and the property (4.3) to set the scale of the field to zero. Putting together (4.22) and (4.23), we obtain for \(r \leqslant t\)

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{l} {\| F_t^{\varepsilon [\ell + 1] (1)} \|_{\mathcal{L}^{(1)}_r (t ; c_{\ell + 1})}}_{}\\ \quad \lesssim C^2 2^{(\alpha - \beta) t} 2^{- \kappa (\ell + 1) t} (\| F^{\varepsilon [\leqslant \ell]} \|_{C, \alpha, \beta, \kappa} + \| F^{\varepsilon [\leqslant \ell]} \|_{C, \alpha, \beta, \kappa}^2) \end{array} \) (4.24)

The bounds (4.21), (4.24) imply that \(\| F^{\varepsilon [\leqslant \ell + 1]} \|_{C, \alpha, \beta, \kappa}\) is finite. This concludes the proof.\(\Box\)

Corollary 4.15. Let \(F^{\varepsilon}\) be the unique solution to the flow equation truncated at \(n\), with the same boundary data as in Theorem 4.13, but in particular \(F^{\varepsilon [0]}_0 (\psi_t) := \lambda (\psi_t)^3\), see eq. (3.27). Let \(H_{> n}^{\varepsilon}\) be defined as in (4.10). Then, there exist constants \(C_0 = C_0 (\alpha, \beta, \gamma, \kappa, n)\) and \(\lambda_0 = \lambda_0 (C)\) independent of \(\varepsilon\) and \(L\) such that, if \(C \leqslant C_0\) and \(\lambda \leqslant \lambda_0 (C)\) then

\(\displaystyle \| F^{\varepsilon} \|_{C, \alpha, \beta, \kappa} \lesssim \lambda, \hspace{4em} \| H_{> n}^{\varepsilon} \|_{C, \alpha, \beta, \kappa} \lesssim_n \lambda^2,\)

uniformly in \(\varepsilon\) and \(L\), where the \(\lesssim\) are up to universal constants.

Proof. The proof is a corollary of the proof of Theorem 4.13.
