If the GBM is indexed by the Hilbert space \(\mathfrak{h}\), with conjugation \(\Theta\), we let \(\mathcal{H} := L^2 (\mathbb{R}; \mathfrak{h})\) and \(P_t := \hat{P}_t \otimes \mathbb{1}\) be an increasing net of projections on it, \(\hat{P}_t\) be the projection of \(L^2 (\mathbb{R})\) onto \(L^2 ([0, t])\).

Definition A.7. Let \(\mathfrak{h}\) be a Hilbert space with conjugation \(\Theta\), let \((\mathcal{H}, (P_t)_{t \geqslant 0})\) be as above and \((\mathcal{M}, \omega, (\mathcal{M}_t)_{t \geqslant 0})\) be its Fock construction. Let \((G_t)_t \subset \mathcal{B} (\mathfrak{h})\) be \(\Theta\)-antisymmetric and differentiable in \(t\) and let \(\dot{G}_t = C_t^{\ast} C_t U_t\) be a decomposition with unitary \(U_t\). Define the processes

\(\displaystyle X_t (f) := a^{\ast} \left( \int_0^t \delta_s \otimes C_s U_s f \mathrm{d} s \right) + a \left( \int_0^t \delta_s \otimes C_s \Theta f \mathrm{d} s \right), \) (A.9)

indexed by \(\mathfrak{h}\).

Remark A.8. Note that if \(f \in \mathfrak{h}\), the functions \(g_t := \int_0^t \delta_s \otimes C_s U_s f \mathrm{d} s\) and \(\tilde{g}_t := \int_0^t \delta_s \otimes C_s \Theta f \mathrm{d} s\) are in \(\mathcal{H}\). In fact, \(\| g_t \|^2_{\mathcal{H}} = \int_0^t \| C_s U_s f \|^2_{\mathfrak{h}} \mathrm{d} s\) and likewise for \(\tilde{g}_t\).

The following proposition completes the proof of Theorem 2.20.

Proposition A.9. The process \((X_t)_{t \geqslant 0}\) defined in (A.9) are a norm-compatible GBM with covariance \(G_t\).

Proof. That \((X_t)_{t \geqslant 0}\) is adapted follows by direct inspection. We prove that it is a martingale, that is, \(\omega_s (X_t (f)) = X_s (f)\) for any \(0 \leqslant s \leqslant t\). For the sake of brevity, we let \(g_{r, t} := \int_r^t \delta_s \otimes C_s U_s f \mathrm{d} s\) and \(\tilde{g}_{r, t} := \int_r^t \delta_s \otimes C_s \Theta f \mathrm{d} s\). We let \(\mathcal{H} = \mathcal{H}_s \oplus \mathcal{H}_{> s}\) and let \(U_s\) be the corresponding fermionic exponential law. Note that \(P_s g_{0, t} = g_{0, s}\) and \(P_s \tilde{g}_{0, t} = \tilde{g}_{0, s}\) We have:

\(\displaystyle U_s X_t (f) U_s^{\ast} = a^{\ast} (g_{0, s}) \otimes \mathbb{1} + \Xi \otimes a^{\ast} (g_{s, t}) + a (\tilde{g}_{0, s}) \otimes \mathbb{1} + \Xi \otimes a (\tilde{g}_{s, t}) .\)

Therefore

\(\displaystyle \omega_s (X_t (f)) = U_s^{\ast} \tilde{\Omega}_{> s} (U_s X_s (f) U_s^{\ast}) U_s = U_s^{\ast} (a^{\ast} (g_{0, s}) \otimes \mathbb{1} + a (\tilde{g}_{0, s}) \otimes \mathbb{1}) U_s = X_s (f) .\)

Next, we prove that it is a centred Grassmann Gaussian process. We begin by checking the anticommutation relations:

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} \{ X_t (f), X_{t'} (f') \} & = & \left\langle \int_0^t \delta_s \otimes C_s \Theta f \mathrm{d} s, \int_0^{t'} \delta_r \otimes C_r U_r f' \mathrm{d} r \right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\\ & & \hspace{3em} + \left\langle \int_0^{t'} \delta_s \otimes C_s \Theta f' \mathrm{d} s, \int_0^t \delta_r \otimes C_r U_r f \mathrm{d} r \right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\\ & = & \int_0^{t \wedge t'} (^{} \langle C_s \Theta f, C_s U_s f' \rangle_{\mathfrak{h}} + \langle C_s \Theta f', C_s U_s f \rangle_{\mathfrak{h}}) \mathrm{d} s\\ & = & {\langle\!\langle} f, G_{t \wedge t'} f' {\rangle\!\rangle}_{\Theta} + {\langle\!\langle} f', G_{t \wedge t'} f {\rangle\!\rangle}_{\Theta} = 0, \end{array}\)

where in the last step we used that \(G_s\) is \(\Theta\)-antisymmetric. Then, we show that \(X_t\) is a centred Gaussian. We compute

\(\displaystyle \left\{ a \left( \int_0^t \delta_r \otimes C_r U_r f \mathrm{d} r \right), X_s (f') \right\} = \int^{t \wedge s}_0 \langle C_r \Theta f, C_r U_r f' \rangle_{\mathfrak{h}} \mathrm{d} r = {\langle\!\langle} f, G_{t \wedge s} f' {\rangle\!\rangle}_{\Theta},\)

hence, the claim:

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} \omega (X_t (f) X_{t_1} (f_1) \cdots X_{t_n} (f_n)) & = & \omega \left( a \left( \int_0^t \delta_r \otimes C_r U_r f \mathrm{d} r \right) X_{t_1} (f_1) \cdots X_{t_n} (f_n) \right)\\ & = & \sum^n_{j = 1} (-)^j {\langle\!\langle} f, G_{t \wedge t_j} f_j {\rangle\!\rangle}_{\Theta} \omega \left( X_{t_1} (f_1) \cdots \not{X_{t_j} (f_j)} \cdots X_{t_n} (f_n) \right) \end{array}\)

Finally, the norm-compatibility is a direct consequence of \(\| a (f) \|, \| a^{\ast} (f) \| \leqslant \| f \|_{\mathfrak{h}}\). This concludes the proof of the proposition.\(\Box\)

Note in particular that Proposition A.9 implies that Definitions 3.18 and 4.27 are meaningful and that we obtain \((X^{L, \varepsilon}_t)_t\) and \((X^{(i)}_t)_t\) simply by plugging \(\mathfrak{C}^{L, \varepsilon}_s\) and \(\boldsymbol{1}_{\mathcal{D}_i} \mathfrak{C}_s\) in place of \(C_s\) in eq. (A.9).

Remark A.10. Let us fix \(\mathfrak{h} := L^2 (\mathbb{R}^d ; \mathbb{C}^n)\) and some unitary \(U\) for concreteness. In this case, we could introduce Grassmann white noise (associated with \(U\) and \(\Theta\)) as the operator-valued distribution

\(\displaystyle \xi (\mathrm{d} s, \mathrm{d} z) := a^{\ast} (\delta_s \mathrm{d} s \otimes U (\cdot, z) \mathrm{d} z) + a (\delta_s \mathrm{d} s \otimes \Theta (\cdot, z) \mathrm{d} z),\)

so that, if \(C_t\) commutes with \(U\) and \(\Theta\), we can write

\(\displaystyle X_t (f) = \int_0^t {\int_{\mathbb{R}^d}}_{} (C_s f) (z) \xi (\mathrm{d} s, \mathrm{d} z) .\)

This actually holds in our setting, see Section 3.3 and clarifies the meaning of Definition 3.18.
